with the -whole-hogger whom I have just cited. Mr. Patterson hesitates not to declare that pure, uplifting thoughts. (the desirableness of which one at once admits, of course) not only feed, but assure all and sundry of a fsweet breath. Here is a characteristic assertion: "It is more certain that malaria proceeds from this atmosphere of anxiolo or evil thought, exprest through impure breath, than from anything that is injurious in the earth's atmosphere." Really, matters have come to such a pass that the one item of breakfast bacon is a considera- tion, and the prospect of being able to live without eating at all is alluring--:-though on second thoughts, alas! ===== 0002601_19150925_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
TESTING FEEDING STUFFS.

The President of the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries desires to call -the attention of agriculturists to the facilities afforded by Local Authorities, under the Fertilisers and Feeding Stuffs Act, 1906, for the sampling and analysis of feeding stuffs purchased for the use of stock. The effect of the European war on the prices and supplies of feeding stuffs and the shortage of skilled labour may result in mistakes and misdescriptions of these materials on the part of sellers. It is therefore very desirable that buyers should avail themselves of the safeguards provided by the Act; whereby a purchaser may have a sample taken and submitted for analysis to the Agricultural Analyst by the Official Sampler appointed by the Local Authority. A small fee is charged by the Local Authority for these services. In order that proceedings may be instituted against the seller in the event of the analysis disclosing an offence under the Act, the sample should be taken within 10 days of the receipt of the goods or the invoice— whichever is the later—and after three clear days' notice to the seller of the intention to take the sample.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
THATCHING THE STACKS.

The objects in thatching should be to cover the stacks so effectually that all rain or snow water may be kept out of the roofs, to do the work with a minimum amount of straw, and to give them an all-round neat appearance. No 'prentice hand can succeed in the work—s skilled workman is needed. The straw should first be straightened out on a door or some- thing of that kind that gives a firm base to work on. It is better if wetted a little, and it is a good plan to lay out the battens to catch any rain that may fall some time before they are wanted, as that saves hauling the water for damping the straw: on thatching day. This work of trimming is part and parcel of the thatching, and is done at the same time. With a very sharp straw or hay knife the ricks should be neatly pared round. Then if the builder has erected his stack properly, and the thatcher has put the thatch on all right, rain, even if it pours down in deluges, will shoot from the roof clear of the walls of the stack. In paring off the thatch at the eaves, the knife should be turned a little upwards, and that gives the right turn to the stream in heavy downpours of rain, and saves the walls froip taking damage. Of course, with ricks that it is ,intended to thresh out in the course of a month or so, one need not go to a lot of trouble and cost in covering and trimming, but the farmer must consider that from one or another cause he may have to hold his ricks over for a longer period. In the latter case there is sure to be a good deal of damage in the roofs, and possibly waterspouts will go right to the bottom if only careless covering has been carried out, Careful thatching generally pays best in the end. Neither is the damage to be reckoned by the harm done to the corn alone, for the straw in these days counts for something for cattle food and other purposes—for nearly as much. indeed, as the grain. But the fodder is not good, if damaged, for anything save litter and, manure.—Warwickshire, in "Farm and Home.'

===== 0002601_19150925_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

WHERE LAND CAN BE FOUND FOR GARDEN PRODUCE.

I would suggest that everyone who has a gar- den should look about and ask himself whether there is any part of the ground which might with advantage be given to food production. in some cases there are areas put to no good use. In others there is a superabundance of meadow which could be brought into cultivation with- out the least disadvantage to the stock. And when I see building land in the neighbourhood of large towns which for many years past has carried nothing but weeds, and is smothked with weeds to-day, then I venture to suggest that there are other directions in which we ean look for increased garden ground.—" AgriVl- tural Economist and Horticultural Review.'

AUTUMN-SOWN ANNUALS.

"It is not for me to judge," he continued. "but I cannot understand the action of those who are opposed to 'National Service. I should have thought that everyone would say that this is a time when, if we are fit and able, we ought to serve our country. We ought not to try to make excuses. I know there ar%those who have to work at home, as well as those who have to fight abroad, but I should have thought that every Man would say to himself, Now is the time when I have to think: Ought I to go? Is it my duty?'" Finally the Rector praised all who had given of their substance in support of the. various war funds. ,

His Honour briefly returned his thanks for the good wishes that had been expressed, remark- ing' that he had listened with pleasure to the kind words which had been uttered. It was quite true that he had had a very long and busy 'experience on the North-Eastern Circuit, where he had been called upon to deal with a great variety of questions. . Perhaps his experience had not been so great in some of the classes of work which came before a county Court Judge as that of some others, but he relied with con- fidence on every assistance being given him by the learned gentlemen practising at the Court, in order that justice might be de both properly and expeditiously.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Shocking Incident.

The sad end of the late stationmaster at Runcorn brought to my mind the circum- stances attending a similar fatality which occurred years ago in a district in which I then lived. On my way to an appointment at the workhouse, I met one of the relieving officers and mentioned to him how I,had just heard that a young woman had been killed by an express at a station crossing some three miles away. I had not then heard the name of the deceased, who proved to be the daughter of the official to whom I had thus without suspecting it announced her death. She was a teacher in a local school, and was intending to take train for home when accidentally caught by a pas- sing express.

Support Homo Industries. Now and for some time to come this should be our motto. Not only should anything made by alien enemies be anathema, but we should not even import from allied countries what can be produced at home. Buying only necessaries, we must see to it that as far as possible our money circulates within our own community. At present this is really vital. At OA recent thrift conference here, one lady made the startling statement that this coun- try had actually spent a million more on for- eign chocolates this year than during the previous twelve months. A little care and thought in our purchases would make a sub- stantial difference to the national income. I often wonder why we buy so much Danish bacon instead of Wiltshire or Irish roll. The latter is superior—otherwise it would not be an offence on the part of the retailer to label the Danish article "Irish " ; indeed, that it is better I can personally testify'

The Kaiser Again. Last week's extracts from an old American newspaper giving some of the German Em- peror's mottoes suggested a dip into "The -House of Hohenzollern," published not very long before the declaration of war, in which Mr. E.A. Brayley Hodgetts pays these among sither compliments to the Kaiser :

"From his mother he has inherited the artistic. gifts for which she was so distin- guished, and that tactfulness, amounting to genius, which his uncle exhibited in so marked a degree. He is. versatile, elo- quent, sagacious, and talented, but he is also diligent, industrious, conscientious, duty-lov- ing, and above all things a soldier. A Hohen- zollern to the finger-tips, he is also essentially a man of his time, modern and up-to-date, who knows how to combine the picturesque features of the Middle Ages with the latest "'discoveries in electricity and chemistry." I should think the writer was ready to kick 'himself soon after the publication of his flat- ter.i4g tribute. To Err is Human.

After all, is there one among us whose sin- lessness entitles him to blame an offender of this class? Consider individual views of the war and the divergent opinions of its various aspects and effects. Take the conscription controversy, for example. Those who avow themselves so strongly in favour of conscrip- tion know no more than the rest of us what information the Government have. And so as to other important questions. lam reminded of a story told in a letter to the Press yeas ago. Let me repeat it here : Once upon a time there was a forest, and in the forest there was a tree, and on the tree there was a branch, and from the branch there hunk a shield (the name of the shield was Ignorance), and one side of the shield was gold (this was the Pathetic aspect of Ignor- ance), and the other side of the shield was silver (this was the Humorous aspect of Ignor- ance). Four knights rode up to the tree from the four points of the compass. The first knight saw the silver side of the shield, and laughed; and the second knight saw the gold, side of the shield, and cried; and the third knight saw first the silver side and laughed and then the gold aide and cried; and the fourth knight, coming up behind the trunk of the, tree, saw neither side of the shield, and neither laughed nor. cried. Then the knight who laughed scoffed at the knight who cried, and the knight who cried scorned the knight who laughed, and the knight who both laughed and cried was amused with both, and the knight who neither laughed nor cried was astonished at all three.

Science of Life and Living. , I always read the "Hints on Health" very soon after opening my weekly "Examiner." I find it interesting to compare opinions ox- pressed therein with those of some other authorities. I well remember the enthusiastic and good-naturedly dogmatic Vicar of Burton- wood insisting in his parish magazine that "the best of all fasts is the daily fast." The Rev. A. M. Mitchell is not only a vegetarian (or ought I to write " fruitarian "?) ; he is a no-breakfaster, holding that to eat no food till midday is the surest road to bodily health, mental power and spiritual devotion. I am not sure, indeed, that the reverend spartan allows himself more than one meal a day. Is it possible to go even one better than that? Mr. Charles Brodie Patterson, a copy of the seventh edition of whose "Dominion and Power" came my way a few years ago, earnestly contends for right breathing. He thinks that so much can be done by breathing that in the far future man may even draw in all his nourishment this way, like plants and many kinds of fishes, and dispense with his digestive organs altogether. He tells us (and this is specially noteworthy in this war time and necessity of thrift) that those who have studied the use and control of the breath without exception eat less, "many reducing thair food by one-half and a few even going beyond that, in every case with beneficial re- sults:" Which provoked the irreverent jour- nalistic comment that after inhaling a nice crisp cutlet of mountain air and a slice or two of ozone pudding, one feels one could do things. 4' Illeatriy and Health." •

This was the sub-headline of one of the aforesaid health " Hints " in these columns last week, and in the paragraph succeeding it Dr. Kellogg had some disquieting things to say about the parlous state physically of un- fortunates afflicted with bad • breath. How- ever. he is mild and gentle in comparison

WHO SHALL APPOINT?

Echoes Of Old Controversy Between Two Committees.

APPEAL TO BOARD OF EDUCAT!ON.

The contrroversy between ,the Health Com- mittee of the Borough and the Education Authority as to which of the two bodies should have the power of appointment of a medical officer whose functitins were purely concerned with the school children of the town, again formed the topic of a debate at the monthly meeting of the Education Committee on Mon- day evening. The discussion arose out of the following minute submitted by the General Pur- poses Sub-committee, and only arrived at a few minutes before the monthly meeting of the com- mittee proper was opened The committee considered the following resolution of the Town Council, dated August 10th, 1915:— The Chairman of the Education Com- mittee and the Director of Education at- tended the [Health] Committee and discussed the question of the appointments in con- nection with the medical inspection of school children. After the deputation had retired a discussion took place and it was resolved - that, it is in the interests of the public health of the town that the appointments be made by the Health Committee as at present, and that it is inadvisable to make any change. It was resolved by the General Purposes Committee that the Chairman of the - Educa- tion Committee be requested to submit the whole facts Of the matter to the Board of Education for their advice and direction. MAYOR WANTS TO AVOID FRICTION.

The Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock) wondered whether it would be advisable or in order, see- ing that the resolution had not been before the Education Committee, to put it to that meeting for confirmation. He only threw out the sug- gestion in order to save the friction which, in his opinion, would undoubtedly follow if a resolution which had been considered so recently as that afternoon was confirmed that evening and acted upon without such members of the committee as might be absent that evening having the opportunity of knowing what the feeling of the General Purposes Committee was, and of saying anything they might desire to say thereon. It was only fair that all the members should be advised as to what minutes were going to be submitted for approval at any par- ticular meeting. IN ACCORD WITH PRECEDENT.

The Chairman (Councillor H. Roberts) said that what it was proposed to do that evening was quite in accordance with precedent, and was perfectly in order. All the members were sum- moned to attend a meeting of the committee, sitting as a General Purposes Committee, and they had attended, or not attended, as they thought fit. It had always been the practice to seek confirmation for the minutes of the General Purposes Committee at the meeting of the Education Committee next following, and that very often happened to be the same day. The members who had not attended had abstained at their own risk and peril, and as the ques- tion involved was not a financial one it did not necessarily form a subject of contention later on.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S AMENDMENT.

The Mayor moved as an amendment that the resolution of the General Purposes Committee be referred back, remarking that he did so in the interests of peace. He thought, even though he might not have been able to attend the meeting of the Genera/. Purposes Com- mittee, that he should have been advised that that important matter was coming up for dis- cussion. He did not agree with the resolution that had, been arrived at. If the Medical In- spection Committee would come forward -with what, in his opinion, was the reasonable sug- gestion that four of their members should be allowed to attend the Health Committee when the matter of appointments affecting the Educa- tion Committee came up, he thought that the suggestion would be accepted by the Health Committee. At any rate, that was his hope. What had at first looked like a little dispute had now grown into a big one, and he could not help tliinking that the Education Com- mittee were acting in a manner that was a little bit childish. (Oh! oh! and laughter.) A sug- gestion had been made by the Chairman of the Education Committee and certain members, but it had not proved acceptable to the Health Committee.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
Is It All a Misunderstanding?

It was quite true that Councillor Roberts had asked the Health Committee not to shut the door upon a settlement, even if they could not accept his suggestion, but for some reason or other the members of the Health Committee had got it into their minds that what the Councillor had laid before them represented the irreducible minimum of what the Medical Inspection Com- mittee wanted. He thought they might put the present position of affairs down to a misunder- standing—a misunderstanding that was really a serious one. Still, he hoped that even at the eleventh hour they might get over the difficulty, and it was with that object in view that he moved his amendment. He did not think that to carry the General Purposes Committee's resolution that night would be fair to the members who had not turned up, but who might have turned up if they had known, specifically, what was going to be placed before the committee.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
MATERIAL FOR THE W.P.8.?

Councillor Archer seconded the Mayor's amendment, remarking that he had been hope- ful that some happy solution to the difficulty would have been found. When one had a pistol pointed at one's head—well, it got rather provoking, to say the least of it. (Laughter.) He thought that the resolution urging that they should go to the Board of Education would be committed to the wastepaper basket by that authority, who did not want to be troubled with such minute details. "I think," the Councillor went on, "that., as the Mayor has said, even at this eleventh hour a solution -can be found, for the reason that I know that many memberi of the Health Committee are in favour of granting this committee some privilege, or making some allowance The Chairman (interrupting): They have a very funny way of showing it. Councillor Archer: I say "some members," but some people say "We will have the sole selection" or "sole authority," and the Health Committee would not have it.

Mr. Upson: We never said it. Councillor Archer: I say the Chairman of the Inspection Committee said it- Mr. Upson: The committee have not said it.

Councillor Archer: I say the Chairman of the Medical Inspection. Committee said: "Unless we have the sole appointment we will either have all or none." They might as well have

===== 0002601_19150925_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 25, 1915.

the whole truth. The dispute had gone on ad nauseam and he hoped that they would have one more try to settle it. As a member of the Health Committee, he would promise to do all in his power to assist in making matters run harmoniously. THE COUNCIL'S REFUSAL.

Mr. J. Upson said that Councillor Archer had not given the committee anything like the whole truth of the matter, nor did he (the speaker) understand what the Mayor meant when he talked about the preservation of peace. If his Worship would carry his mind back, he must know that when the present difficulty first arose all sorts of discussions took place, and suggestions were made as to how a way out of the difficulty could .be found. At length it was decided that a deputation, consist- of Mr. H. Kingdon, Mr. C. E. Parker and himself, should meet the Town Council, in General Purposes Committee assembled. That meeting duly came off and various methods were discussed and various suggestions made with a view to a compromise along the lines of which they coUld possibly continue to work. But the Council refused, absolutely, to meet them in any particular. After that meeting the Chairman of the Medical Inspection Sub- committee said: "We must accepl the position and bury the,hatchet," and the sub-committee were going on with their work, hoping that the Health Committee would—when the next appointment affecting them came to be made— ask some of the members of the sub-committee to come into consultation with them before they made an appointment affecting the school work. Well, time went on, and another official was appointed by the Health Committee.

To all the officials individual tributes and thanks were accorded for their able and untiring services.

SHOULD COMMITTEE RETIRE? Mr. J. Sephton expressed an opinion that they should have a change on the committee—at least two to retire every year to give the younger members a chance. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Walter Dennett said that, after the glow- ing tributes which had been paid to the com- mittee. he did not think it would be advisable to make a change at this juncture. • Tho Chairman: I should suggest that the names of anybody you suggest going on should be sent in to the committee before the annual meeting.—Later Mr. Atkin added that Mr. Sephton's suggestion was quite a good one, but it was always difficult to say on the spur of the moment to a man who had served them well, -" You shall go off," so, of course, the matter came up a little suddenly. He could only say that not one of the gentlemen on the committee would stand in the way of any young man serv- ing on it. Everybody in the room, he thought, was in agreement with Mr. Sephton, but appar- ently it would be necessary to alter the rules and that could not be done without a fortnight's notice.

The matter was accordingly left over until the next annual meeting.

"NOBLE AND PATRIOTIC."

The Chairman, in thanking the members for the honour they had accorded, him by inviting him there that night, and also by previously having made him one of their vice-presidents, said he had very deeply at heart the welfare of the Musical Society. From the reports of their secretary and treasurer he saw absolutely noth- ing at all with which they could find fault, and the one point which he wanted particularly to emphasise was that there must be hundreds of .people in Warrington who looked upon what the society had done in the past with the utmost admiration.

Their committee some fourteen or fifteen months ago were faced with a very difficult question: Could they hold the society together— having already spent some money in prepara- tion for their opera? Well, the figures spoke for themselves. They had not worked for profit this season; they had not tried to work for them- selves. They had kept the society going at a loss, and yet had behaved in such a noble and patriotic spirit! For the sake of them all in- dividually, and for the sake of the whole society's future, it certainly should redound to their credit that they had sacrificed themselves in the matter of expenses to the extent of a loss of .£l6. On the other hand. the return for four patriotic charities, they had handed over nearly .2100

===== 0002601_19150925_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
Did The Health Committee Over-ride?

And no consultation was held with the Medi- cal Inspection Sub-committee. Another ap- pointment fell vacant and the matter was similarly dealt with by the Health Committee, and then, ultimately, the position of an officer who had been appointed by the Medical In- spection Sub-committee became empty. Then the bother arose. Without consultation at all with the Medical Inspection Committee, the Health. Committee appointed a dentist; so that it could not be said that in any one particular had the Health Committee yielded one jot or tittle to the reasonable demands of the Medical Inspection Sub-committee. The proposals for compromise had all been on the part of the Medical Inspection Sub-committee, and in- variably they had been met with a blank refusal. The Health Committee had been absolutely unyielding from the beginning, whereas the Medical Inspection Sub-committee had sought every channel with a view to arriving at a working compromise on the ques- tion. He joined in the hope that even at the eleventh hour some way out of the difficulty might be found, and he believed that that way out would be found along the lines of a decision given by the Board of Education, as suggested in the resolution that night.

DEPUTY-MAYOR IN A MINORITY OF ONE

The Deputy-Mayor said he was afraid he could not vote for the Mayor's amendment. lie had no doubt that some of the members of the Medical Inspection Sub-committee would re- member that he made a certain suggestion for a compromise; indeed, he had always been :n favour of a compromise, but he had, unfor- tunately he thought, hitherto, generally, been in a minority of one.

AT THE - THIRTEENTH HOUR!

The Vice-Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) recalled that on a previous occasion he had taken a line somewhat similar to that which the Mayor had taken but he had failed to get a seconder: he proposed that they should not go to the nationat authority. It seemed to him that in the matter of this controversy they had passed the eleventh hour and had, in fact, got to the thirteenth hour; wherefore he sympathised with the remarks that the Deputy-Mayor had made, and adumbrated the somewhat unfair treatment to which that gentleman had been treated by his colleagues on the Health Cotuthittee.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAIRMAN'S EFFORTS FOR PACIFIC SETTLEMENT.

The Charrman said that voicing, as he thought and still believed, the conditions which the Education Committee would have been glad to have agreed to, he pledged his word to stand by the provisions in dispute, if suitable to the Health Committee: Fortified by the feeling that, with regard to one of the most important suggestions, he would have a strong supporter in the Deputy-Mayor, he went to the Health Com- mittee full of hope that the matter in dispute would be settled in a way that would leave no sting behind it, and would reflect no humiliation on either the Health Committee or the Educa- tion Committee. He never in his life strove more earnestly to effect a pacific settlement than he did in this matter. He carefully studied the words and expressions which lie used on the occasion of the interview, and the Chairman of the Health Committee said to him: "Are your proposals the minimum which your committee would accept ?" and he replied that he would be very sorry to say that. If what he had sug- gested did not commend itself in any particular to the Health Committee he hoped that they would give him a further opportunity of meet- ing them for the purpose of discussing the 'matter with them, because the Education Com- mittee were prepared to make any sacrifice of time or effort to find a happy solution to the difficulty. What was the result? Committee's Proposals " Kicked Out."

The Education Committee's proposals were absolutely kicked out. He would think twice before he went cap-in-hand to the Health Com- mittee in a matter of that sort again. In his opinion no harm could come from an appeal to the Board of Education. If the Board said to the committee "You are wrong and the Health Committee are right" they would accept the decision as loyal men, and abide by it. If, on the other hand, the Board said "The Education Committee are right in their con- tention," then public opinion would be in- fluenced by that fact a,nd, moreover, he believed that certain members of the Health Committee who, at present, believed that the power of appointment devolved upon them, would come and vote for a more reasonable solution. No- body could possibly be harmed by adopting the committee's proposal.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DIVISION.

Only three votes were cast in favour of the amendment for referring the matter back. The amendment was therefore declared lost and the original proposition, embodied in the minute printed above, was carried, the meeting then terminating.

"PRAISE FAMOUS MEN."

The Rector's Opinion Of Mr. Lloyd George.

The Rector (the Rev. Canon Willis) preaching at the Warrington Parish Church on Sunday, on the subject "Let us now praise famous men," referred to some prominent Biblical in- stances and the rulers and commanders in the countries of our Allies who are engaged in the work of shaping the destinies of civilisation. We ought, he 'said, to be thankful we had such a monarch as King George, who was very quiet and modest, but was doing his best for his country. The "King, like his cousin the Czar, was a very religious man, and trusted in God. Among the statesmen to whom the Rector alluded. was Mr. Lloyd George, whom he described as a very brave man. He confessed that in pre-war days he did not admire Mr. Lloyd George, but new, he said, "I think, he is one of the finest British characters we have —a man who is .nover afraid to speak out, and who, in addressing large bodies of working men, does not hesitate to tell them the truth. We ought" to. be very proud of him." The Prime Minister, Sir Edward Grey, Mr. Balfour, Mr. Winston Churchill, Lord Kitchener, Sir John French and Sir John Jellicoe were praised, and the Rector passed on to acknowledge the great debt which we owe to our soldiers and sailors. National Service.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Patriotic Work And A Splendid

Result.

C 0 M Pvl ITTEE QUESTION.

Taking everything into consideration I think we have come out remarkably well.

As expressed by the Secretary, Mr. Lionel Fairclough, such was the general verdict upon the past season's work voiced at the Warring- ton Musical Society's general meeting, which followed the resumption of practices in the Cairo-street School on Tuesday evening. In the absence of Sir Gilbert Greenall—whohad been unexpectedly called to London—Mr. W. Ernest Atkin took the chair.

The lion. Secretary, in giving his report, stated that in common with most institutions the war had played havoc with their original plans, and they had had to make a complete change of programme. Considerable progress had been made Avith the opera, "Rip Van Winkle," which they had purposed to perform in October last year, but under the circum- stances it was decided to abandon the opera alto- gether for the time • being. However, the work that had been done would doubtless be a great help when they could once more begin to think about operas. War Charities Concerts.

The Parr Hall was not available at all for the last three months of the previous year, but the Rector very kindly gave them permission to give a performance of "Elijah" in the Parish Church; and he (the secretary) thought it was a matter upon which they could congratulate themselves that at that performanCe all. the solos were taken by their own members. The collection taken in the church realised 10s. 7d., and this was handed over to the special War Fund of the Infirmary to provide a new ward for wounded soldiers. (Applause.) By the courtesy of the. Mayor and Corpora- tion, they were enabled to have the use of the Parr Hall free for the second and last concert, held on March 20th. The proceeds amounted to £3l is. 11d., which they divided equally between the local War Relief Fund and the Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital. This con,cert was of a patriotic character, and for a soloist they had the pleasure of a very fine Belgian 'cellist— Monsieur Henri van Damme. Chorus Statistics. The strength of the .chorus was:

• Previous year. Sopranos Altos Tenors . 23 ... 20 Basses

159 161 so that with the exception of the basses—of whom a considerable number had entered the Army—the strength of the chorus was prac- tically the same as in the previous year. The attendance at rehearsals was fair, the sopranos having the best percentage with 64 per cent.; the average was about 59 per cent. Standard of Orchestra.

The strength of the orchestra was 35 per cent., against 38 the season before; and the standard of their work could be judged by the fact that the professional help they received was exceed- ingly low. They would need to go a long way back to find a season when the expenses under this head had been so_ small. Everything considered, the society had come out splendidly, especially when they took into account that about £l7 which had been spent in preparation for the opera was absolutely lost, for the time being, at any rate. He regretted exceedingly to report that within the last two or three weeks the society had lost one of its oldest and most valued members—Mr. Scotson, who had belonged to it for over forty years; a ,wonderful reader and a hard worker. The practices he missed (until the last two seasons, when his health was not so good) were very few indeed, and in- that respect he was an example to all of them. He needed the prac- tices probably less than anybodyt yet he rarely missed. (Hear, hear.) The report was unanimously adopted, the Chairman remarking that he was sure the season had been a trying one for every official, hut cir- cumstances had turned out very satisfactory.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
PAID THEIR WAY.

Mr. John P. Reynolds (the hon. treasurer) then presented the balance sheet. This, showed that the expenses incident to the Park concert amounted to £2 19.5. 50., which the society paid —the £27 16s. 4d. realised going to the Infirmary. Of the "Elijah" concert, the expenses totalled .£ll 15s. Bd., and £29 10s. 7d. went as a War Ward donation to the Infirmary, as the result of the church collection. The members kindly contributed £5 2s. lid. towards the expenses. The society consequently paid ..t6 las. 44d loss on this concert.

In the Patriotic Concert the expenses amounted to £3l 17s. 9d., and .215 lls, each was donated to the Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital and the Local War Fund; the income from sale of tickets and programmes left a loss balance only of 4s. 11d. In the outside sale of these tickets the members raised £l7 55. 6d. and the Working Men's Committee £25 7s. 6d. The members' subscriptions (excluding those specially for the " Elijah " concert) , realised £44 12s. ed., and other subscriptions £35 15s. Under the circumstances, Mr. Reynolds said, these subscriptions were very satisfactory. They were in a great difficulty, being unable to give the regular concerts, and it was then decided to give two performances for War Funds and to circularise the subscribers and ask ,them to do what they could to help. The pre-war ex- penditure on opera and bank interest together made ..tl6 19s. 6d. ,On the season's work the net loss was £l2 Bs. 6d.

The Rector's Willingness.

Perhaps the most satisfactory part about it all was that it was really done without any loss; for if they took out the loss at the beginning on account of the opera—for which they got no return—that amount practically balanced the loss on the season. With regard to the present season, it seemed probable that they would have to work much on the same lines as last, for it seemed hopeless to carry on the concerts under present conditions as before the war. He congratulated the members on the " Elijah " performance; it was in more ways than one a very great success. He had heard "Elijah " many times, but none had appealed to him as the performance in the Parish Church had done. The Rector also had expressed himself as much pleased with the performance and with the quiet, orderly way in which it was conducted and all arrangements carried out. He (the Rector) had further intimated his willingness that a similar performance of the "Messiah" about Christmas should be given. This (Mr. Reynolds said) would, of .course, be duly considered and dealt with by the com- mittee to be shortly elected. In concluding Mr. -Reynolds added his tribute to the memory of the late Mr. Sedtson. He had known him for many years, when his brother (Mr. 11. W. Reynolds) was secretary and secretary and treasurer. During this period Mr. Scotson for a good many years acted as assistant secretary, and gave much time and trouble for the welfare of the society. He thought the society had lost a very great friend. The accounts were passed nem con.

Educating in High Art.

He had always found that, troubled as its career was, the Warrington Musical Society came out smiling, and it had been a very dark time. It was not a money-making concern; and they had kept together educating in high art a great number of members. And—according to an old Latin tag—by their faithful study of that high art they had shown that what was known as German Kultur did not stand any chance in Warrington. The society bad ex- celled itself in the willingness and unselfishness with which it had worked, and the dominant note had been "excellent good fellowship." (Ap- plause.) WORKING MEN'S COMMITTEE THANKED. On Mr. Reynolds's motion, hearty thanks were accorded to Mr. J. H. J. Hampson (secretary of the Infirmary) and the Working Men's Com- mittee for their assistance on the occasion of the patriotic concert held in the Parr Hall.

MR. CROSSLEY ON THE OUTLOOK.

• Mr. F. H. Crossley (the conductor) prefaced his remarks by endorsing everything that had been said in regard to their late friend, Mr. Scotson ; his death came quite as a shock to him (the speaker). He knew what a great help Mr. Scotson always was to his fellow members, giv- ing them confidence by his excellent reading. His death was a great loss to the society. After referring to the early . effect of the national. crisis upon their arrangements, Mr. Crossley pointed out that although their pre- arranged programme was "knocked on the head," the members had as a matter of fact done more work than usual—as they took part in four concerts during the season. The orches- tra also did a great deal of work at Sir Arthur H. Crosfield's concert on behalf of the Belgian Belief Fund; while the chorus took part in Madame Clara Butt's concert. That did not come in the report, of course. So that what had promised to be a very unsettled season turned out a very successful one, and he could only hope for as much success this season. " Morrie England " and " Messiah."

It was impossible to say exactly what the programme for the coming season would be. They had been thinking of giving selections from "Morrie England" at a patriotic concert early in November; these would not take up quite al! the programme; there. would also be room for a few more items. He thought it Would be an excellent thing to take up Mr. Reynolds's hint about a performance of "The Messiah" in the Parish Church at Christmastide; it would re- .quire a little arrangement, but he was sure they would all enjoy it very 'much. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion Mr. Crossley paid high tribute to the work of the officials—particularly to the untiring and efficient service of Mr. W.- H. Payton as organist and accompanist, and to the regular and quiet work of the librarian, Mr. P. Walsh. (Applause.) On the motion of, Mr. W. H. Payton, seconded by Mrs. Willett, very hearty thanks were accorded to Mr. Atkin for 'taking the chair. The meeting was attended by a large propor- tion of the members.

NEW COUNTY COURT JUDGE.

Bar's Welcome And Good Wishes. Warrington's new County Court Judge—His Honour J. Scott Fox, K.C.—took his seat for. the first time ,at the local Court on Thursday morning, when he was cordially welcomed by members of the Bar.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
A LONG AND WIDE EXPERIENCE.

• Speaking on behalf of the barristers practis- ing at the Court, Mr. E. Acton, of Manchester, said he desired to utter a few words of very hearty and respectful welcome. They knew that his Honour was the leader of the Bar on the North-Eastern Circuit, and they of the Northern Circuit were proud to remember that there was a time when the two circuits were one. Therefore they of the Northern Circuit welcomed his Honour almost as one of them- selves. Moreover, they welcomed him more especially because he brought to, that Court— and to the other Courts over which he would preside—a long and wide experience of the great industrial centres of the North—the busy towns of Yorkshire, which had so many points in common with the industrial centres of Lanca- shire, with which his Honour would now have to deal. He desired on behalf of the Bar not only to say how heartily they welcomed his Honour but how sincerely they hoped that there might, under his jurisdiction, be main- tained between Bench and Bar those cordial and friendly relations which made the business of the .Court so much more pleasant and effective. •

MORE WHEAT, LESS

BARLEY.

The preliminary statements of areas under crops and the number of live stock on agri- cultural holdings, as returned in June, 1915, enable the Board of Agriculture to give a summary for the United Kingdom, with the comparative figures for 1914. • The acreage under wheat in 1915 was 2,334,090 against 1,904,930 in 1914, an increase on the year of 429,160 acres, or 22.5 per cent.

The acreage under - oats advanced from 3,877,960 to 4,148,050, an increase of 270,090 acres, or 7.0 per cent. There was. an increase of land under potatoes of 5,510 acres—from 1,197,010 to 1,202,520, an increase of 0.5 per cent. All other crops showed decreases, barley by 347,190 acres; turnips and swedes by 135,110 acres; mangels by 15,370 acres; clover and sain- foin by 62,980 acres; and permanent grass for hay 69,520 acres. It will be seen that the increase in wheat and- oats is largely discounted by a falling off in other crops, such as barley, beans, peas and turnips, also that instead of an increase of arable land there is a further decrease of 32,610 acres.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
The Brighter Side.

There is nothing very satisfactory in these facts, and we must turn to the figures dealing with live stock for the brighter side of the picture. The decrease of 8 per cent. in the total number of horses, says "Farm and Rome," is no greater than was expected in view of the drain upon them for Army purposes, and the position in their case is quite exceptional. Cattle have beaten all previous records and show an increase in their total of 3 per cent. for the• year.. The great decrease in cows and heifers in-milk or in-calf is the only dark spot, and points to a shrinkage in dairy farm- ing and the slaughter of breeding animals owing to the high price of beef. The great in- crease is found in yearlings and calves, which is both significant and highly satisfactory. There is no doubt now abont the existence of a great movement in favour of more extensive weaning and rearing. The increase in sheep is smaller, being only 11 per cent., but this is evidently owing to the meagre fall of lambs last spring, seing that the number of those "under one year" show a decrease of over 100,000, yet the balance of totals is on the right side. Swine have decreased by 21 per cent., which with the prices that have ruled for pork and bacon comes rather as a surprise. The only apparent cause for the decrease is the high price of feeding stuffs.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====

I-DTATO DISEASE.

Favoured by moist weather in August, potato. disease (Phytophthora infestans), has destroyed the haulm of potato crops in-many parts of the country. The dead haulm, if allowed to lie on the soil, may infect the tubers. In view, how- ever, of the drieV weather of the past -few weeks, it is probable that as yet relatively few tubers have been attacked, and, consequently, tiat the immediate remoxal of diseased haulm would minimise risk of infection. As a further precaution, however, all crops should be lifted as soon as possible and carefully stored. Further particulars of this disease, with

===== 0002601_19150925_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL CONFERENCE.

At the Church House, Bridge-street, on Wed- nesday afternoon, strong addresses in opposition to the Government's gradual closing of Church schools were delivered to a meeting of the Educational Council of the Ruri-Decanal Con- ference. The Rev. and Hon. C. F.. Cross (rural dean) presided.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
"RELIGIOUS EDUCAT:ON NEEDED."

The Rev. W. J. Elsley (vicar of St. Ann's, Stanley)—having emphasised the importance of the Church taking a greater interest in the subject—said one of the most important ques- tions in Church life to-day was the child. Even many boys and girls still at school were apparently uncontrolled, and warranted religious training side lby side with the secular. Police reports also showed an increase of offences amongst children under 16—in Liver- pool, for instance, an increase of over 200 a year for several years. How best could they main- tain definite religious teaching within the reach of every child of every stage of society, where required? They meant to insist that if un- denominationalists had rights Churchmen had rights too. Schools continued to be closed, lnd they must think whether new methods ,of the Board of Education did not require new methoas of defence. (Hear, hear.) They could not shut their eyes to the fact that ucarly every- thing depended upon the headmaster, who might be an unbeliever. The Sunday, school Gould do much, but it was quite impr)ssible on one day of the week to supply volan:As rily what should be a regular feature of lie other six also. The training colleges built by the Church for Church teachers seemed to be ?oring their support—often due to the way in whi4t many people of all classes send their children to Roman Convents.

Central -Body Needed. Were the Churchpeople so indifferent? On the other hand there were many diocesan societies in all parts of the country doing ex. cellent work! What they lacked_ was a strong central institution that would connect them all and make them a power with the Govern- ment. New• regulations regarding school accommodation aimed a severe blow at their schools in the cities, where land was scarce; practically all would be classed as ' inefficient ' and closed. Meanwhile the Education Rate would be doubled, and the ratepayers would dis- cover the reason too late. , The Government knew the weakness of the isolated schools, and acted accordingly. As a result of the change coming over the colleges, teachers were heing sent into schools to give religious instruction with insufficient training. If the Church was to make headway she must have-a constructive policy. Catholics Criticised.

4.••••..••••••• The danger of Anglican parents sending their children to Roman Convents could not be underestimated; because althoUgh the sisters might not tamper with the faith of the chil- dren, the atmosphere and friendships must have some influence over their minds. The Church would have.to fight if she was to hold her own; and she would win the day. The present system of education was altogether un- satisfactory. The Chairman, remarked that all of them, though "of little statute," could make the best of their ability and power. They in this neigh- bourhood would do well to remember the great leader they had in this line in Archdeacon Maitland Wood. (Hear, hear.)

" NOT DEALT WITH FAIRLY." „The Rev. John H. Thorpe (vicar of St. ‘jeorge's, Stockport, and rural dean and ex- chairman and press secretary of the Church Schools Emergency League) said Churchmen in the past had been just the one religious body least prepared and inclined to fight for their rights. Having regard to the entirely altered conditions of political life, the Church would have to exercise her influence in such a way as to let the electorate know her case and see that consideration was given to it. They must attack the reasons their opponents had given for attacking them. They must teach to their children the faith of the parents; they desired for instance that Roman Catholics should have the right, and also the Nonconformists. But the Church had not been dealt with fairly; he spoke mainly of the elementary schools, but it also applied to the secondary schools—which were more inportant since the influential classes- ef the future were being educated there! The children daily attending Church schools numbered 2,132,000; those daily attending elementary Council schools 3,214,300. Of 'course all attending the Church schools were not Church children, but those who were Noncon- formists were protected by the "conscience clause," and the number withdrawn wits so small as to be negligible.

Cowper-Temple Clhuse.

The question was the special business of the clergy, the parents of the children, the God- parents of the children, and the manages of the schools. Education, if it was without the. fear of God at the beginning, was useless. This was their programme :Attack the Cowper-Temple Clause in the Education Act of 1870 introduced by Mr. Balfour. This clause said: "No religious catechism or religious formulary which is distinctive of any particular denomina- tion shall be taught in the [Council] school." (1) This made no provision at all for giving any religious instruction, though he was sure the framers did not mean that none should be given. Unfortunately when the Act was passed it was the letter which ruled. Local option in the matter of religious instruction could never be accepted by Churchpeople. (2) The clause said that if religious instruction was given at all it was not to• be given in the best way. (3) It made no provision for the teacher, and the more earnest a teacher was who did not belong to the Church the more harm he would be doing the child. There could be no settlement of the education question in England till the Cowper-Temple clause was abolished! On the motion of the Rev. W. J. Stanton (Thelwall), seconded by the Rev. P. It. Gillespy, hearty thanks were accOrded the speakers.

to a much greater extent than our womenfolk, who, however, are always faithful to the black and white of the shepherd. This, like the Royal Stuart in Paris, is seen here all the year round, and is made in materials of every description. Tartan skirts are fashioned as nearly as pos- sible to resemble the soldiers' kilts. Very few are pleated to the Waist. There is a well-fitted yoke, moulded to the figure, and to this the lower part of the skirt is attached. Footwear and hosiery must be in keeping with a tartan suit, and the most appropriate shoes are brogues. The stockings, of course, match the dress exactly. A Scotch tartan suit is an economical purchase, for it is good in quality, and very durable-

Hosiery And Underwear. Some of the new hosiery in check and tartan designs is more startling than artistic. It is mostly of American origin, and is woven to match in colour the shades of the tweeds to be worn with it. Not only are gloves, hosiery and underwear coming from America, but also from Japan. The supplies from America cannot be sold at the price of the German, Austrian or Belgian makes, but those from Japan are cheaper, and ...compare favourably, so it is said, as regards quality and appearance with Conti- nental goods. British manufacturers are also successfully turning out fabric gloves and all descriptions of woven underwear, and seeing our former dependence on Germany, Austria, Bel- gium and France for this kind of clothing, it is remarkable that the trade has seemingly been so little affected by the war.

Trimmed Armholes. Armholes are receiving much attention, since so many afternoon and other frocks are sleeve- less. Princess dresses and those of the jumper style rarely have sleeves to match the materials used in their confection. Georgette has been very largely used with taffetas in this connection, since the two fabrics combine excellently; it also flatters the arms, producing a misty, cloudy effect. It will probably be as popular during the autumn and winter as it has proved throughout the spring and summer. There are many way of trimming armholes. Some are outlined with bands of the dress material, others are embroidered by hand with silk and wool, whilst fur, very narrow in width will be very much

The remains of boiled or steamed fish, lib. of potatoes to 11b. of fish, loz. of butter, some white sauce, and a teaspoonful of anchovy essence. See that the fish is free from skin and bone, mix it with the white sauce and anchovy essence and put it in a greased pie-dish. Boil the potatoes until soft, beat them well with a _

little bufter, pepper, salt, and milk. Place this on the fish, rough the top with a fork, and bake in a hot oven until the potato is slightly brown.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Women As Dispensers. Many notable women took part in the dis- cussion on educational work at the British Association meeting, and debates on other sub- jects go to prove that chemistry offers the educated woman an interesting and well-paid career. It is, moreover, a very suitable pro- fession, requiring a talent for detail which most women possess. The War Office employs a number of qualified women dispensers in military hospitals, and there is an increasing demand for their services. The hours are not excessive, and seldom exceed seven in the day's work. Examination results show that women

'lave been very successful in the study of chemistry, and several girl students have lately won important pharmaceutical scholarships in open competition with men.

Woman's Efficiency. It seems strange that men should invariably have been employed in all furniture depart- ments of the big stores and shops, to dust and clean the stock. Now that seemingly purely domestic " job " is performed by women who have proved so efficient and intelligent that the managers say they will not again employ men for the purpose. At the bookstalls, girls are a great success. They fully recognise that they are fulfilling a patriotic duty. The same may be said of the women ticket collectors, and tram conductors, whO, when of a superior class, do the work just as well as men. There is a wide scope for the further employment of women on railways, where much of the work is well within their capacity. Tartans And Plaids.

Tartans generally synchronise with the egrunoxes, but the war probably accounts for their unusual popularity this autumn. They have, however, merits all their own, for there is something very fascinating about them.' ,The dark background throws up most effectively the gay colouring of the stripes and crossbars. American women and Parisiennes favour plaids

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful

Suggestions.

Pickled Crab-Apples.

Select perfect fruit, wash and remove the blossom ends and stems; boil gently in water ,to cover in which you have sliced half a lemon. Lay in a colander or on a sieve to drain. Put I one quart of good cider vinegar in a preserving I kettle with two pounds of granulated sugar and I when sugar is dissolved boil ten minutes. Put the apples in glass jars and pour the syrup over them and seal. If you desire, add a few .whole spices tied up in little cheesecloth bags, remov- ing these before putting the fruit in the jars.

Peas and Rice. This is excellent at this season, when peas are still fresh and green. In 2oz. of dripping or butter fry two tablespoonfuls of finely- chopped onion, one dessertspoonful of chopped parsley, and a little lemon thyme and mint, also chopped. When well browned, add a quart of boiling water, 4oz. of butter, 2oz. of grated cheese, :11b. •of green peas, 4oz. of unpolished rice, and salt and pepper to taste. Stew over a slow fire until peas ,and rice are cooked, fre- quently shaking the an to prevent burning, and adding a little more water as the vegetables absorb that in the pan. When cooked serve in a hot-dish, sprinkle grated cheese on top, and send to -table with good brown gravy.

How to Cook Cabbage. Many French people, Spaniards and Argen- tines, are most particular about the preparation of cabbage. The French custom is to throw the cabbage (all leaves separated from the stalk and loose) into a saucepan of boiling water, which sends it off the boil. They wait till it has returned to boiling point, boil the cabbage for one minute, drain off the water completely, and throw it away. This makes it more easily digestible. They then allow the cabbage to cook in the "pot-au-feu," or make " soupe au chou" or put it on as a vegetable, "conservatively," in a small amount of water, not nearly covering the leaves, which they press down with a wooden spoon until they are tender. The cook- ing will take from 20 to 40 minutes, according to the age of the cabbage. When almost cooked they drain off the liquor (which can be added to soup or stock), turn out on a board, chop it like spinach, and return to the saucepan to make it hot with butter. The Argentine custom is to quarter the cabbage and scald in the same way before cooking it with all the other vegetables. Lemon Sole au Gratin With Tomato Sauce.

One lemon sole, a slice of onion, a few button mushrooms, a teaspoonful of lemon juice, breaderumbs, a dessertspoonful of butter, salt,

BIG COMPETITION.

IVliich Nephew. And Niece Can Get

Most New Members?

MY DEAR GIRLS AND BOYS,--

I have got a fine idea! It has just struck me slap bang in the thinking-box, and you don't know how proud I am of it. Fancy your poor old Uncle Ned getting a good idea! You know, usually old people don't get them—or if they do they keep them to themselves. But this is such a very fine one that I simply must tell it you; because, of course, I like always to sharethe fun with my loving nephews and nieces. . "What is this great idea?! I hear you ask anxiously.

Well, it is this: I am going to give a prize to the boy and a prize to the girl who gets most new members for Our Corner in a week. Now of course that will be giving you only a short time in which to do it; but, then, splendid things can often be • done in a hurry, and we shall like it all the better because you won't have to wait a long time for the results. Now to-dayris Saturday, and you have got till a week on Monday to get your new members. As soon as you get a friend' to promise to join. don't wait a single minute, but write his (or her) name and your name and pop them into the letter-box straight off. The girls can get boy members, and the boys girl members, of course. Only don't forget to put in their addresses, what- ever you do! I wonder what will be the biggest number I get? You can be sure that all week I shall be kneeling behind the door counting the letters as Mr. Postman drops them into the box; and then hurriedly sorting them out. " Aha! Another member from Dolly Daintytoes," I shall be say- ing; or "Good luck! Two more from Tommy Sucktoffee !" I am going to write them in a special book, and keep it by my Big Book in the beautiful inlaid mahogany case that stands by the desk on that lovely spot where the carpet has got worn through and the hole wants hid-

New York's Fat Women.

"There are more fat women to the square mile in New York than in any other city of the United States," said a. dealer in an anti-fat preparation. "I think Philadelphia comes next, then Boston, and last of all Chicago. How do I account for so much obesity? Well, I suppose because this is a city of such long dis- tances and there 'are so many surface and ele- vated car lines in all directions that women get into the habit of riding wherever they go: They soon become lazy and think they will feel fatigued if they walk six blocks. Then they eat heartily and are too fond of sweetmeats. One day it gradually dawns upon them that they are growing abnormally fat and they wonder what they shall do to reduce their weight. They don't like to fast, so they go through a course of gymnastics to take off the superfluous flesh. If such exercises fail to re- duce their proportions they come to) us and take our remedies. You would be surprised to see how many stout young women come here for our cure. The fair sex seem to be more worried over being fat than men, though I think of the two a fat woman never looks so bad as a fat man, because her clothes help to hide her ungraceful proportions."

My word! it was a narrow escape, though,. wasn't it?

used. Marabout sometimes takes the place of fur. ,Touches of silk and wool embroidery, that can be easily and quickly made at home are used on collars and cuffs by tailors to give indi- viduality and distinction to suits and tailored gowns-of a severe type. Autumn Hats.

French sailor hats, large in size, continue to be very popular, probably because they appear to suit faces of almost every type, and to look well with every style of coiffure. In white velour, trimmed with a broad band of corded ribbon drawn up in front with two 'big loops, this type of hat is most becoming and attrac- tive, the velvet lining of the brim softening the otherwise hard effect of the shape. Of course the latest novelty is the high crown hat, that closely resembles the coachman's "topper" of a hundred years ago. Some milliners predict a vogue for it, but others are doubtful of its success. A shape modelled on the lines of& a Belgian officer's cap is among the novelties. It is made of (hill black satin, and has an aluminium tassel in front. The new hats are frequently trimmed towards the back. Ostrich feathers are a favourite trimming, and are often placed under the brim. Veils are frequently dispensed with altogether. Borders an inch in depth are a feature, and with an open blbuse, the veil should be long enough to encircle the neck with one fold of several thicknessaii and to admit of being fastened behind.

Honey As Food. It is a matter for regret that so little use i. made of honey. Since sugar has been imported in enormous quantities, the demand for honey has gradually ceased, but on the Continent it is still utilised in very many ways. Honey cake, bread and dainties of all kinds are made in every country of Europe, save in these Isles. The ancients esteemed it a valuable and agree- able food, that enriched the system. purified the blood, and guaranteed immunity from plague and other epidemics. It might well take the place of margarine, where that commodity is eaten as a substitute for butter. Used economi- cally, it costs no more, and supplies far more body fuel and energy. On slices of bread, an ounce of honey goes as far as the same quantity of jam, where it can be obtained cheaply. It may be substituted for sugar with rice, sago, &c., and with baked apples.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

A Fool's Paradise,

Ask any man why he smokes, and the only answer he will allege is that it soothes him. As if any healthy man needed soothing all day long! And if he needs it for his troubles. and worries, what about his wife? Has she no need of being soothed into partial forgetfulness of her, worries? But how horrified he would be at the mere suggestion of her smoking. And, after all, what is meant by his being soothed ? Does it not mean that he is narcotised, stupified into a condition practically similar to that of the alcoholic—that his brain and nervous sys- tem are for the time being paralysed, ancesthet- ised, so that he is no longer able to feel or see things as they really are, but has been drugged into a Fool's Paradise of unreality? A Fool's Paradise indeed! For who but a fool would believe that he can purchase artificial relief from brain or muscle weariness or distress, without having to pay for it in, some way ?—J. Johnson, M.D., in "Good Health."

Clothing the Child.

Considerable wisdom is needed properly to adapt an artificial covering to body require- ments in a manner that will promote the com- fort and health of the wearer. Clothing in itself furnishes no heat. It keeps us warm by retaining the natural heat of the body. The strata of warm air next to the body inside the clothing is the real protection. Each added garment provides an additional "coat" of air about the body—for which reason several thin garments are much warmer than a single thick one. Mothers who bear this fact in mind and dress their little ones accordingly find it less difficult to keep them equably clothed. Slight changes of apparel suited to the needs of vary- ing temperatures can he easily made by remov- ing a thin garment or adding one, as conditions require, whereas a complete disrobing for change of clothing might seem burdensome. The amount of clothing needed to keep the body at the uniform temperature for health must, of course, vary with the weather and conditions of living. That it is not possible to gauge this need altogether by the season will be evident to any thoughtful person. A thunder shower in spring or summer may lower the temperature several degrees. At any season a cold day may succeed a hot one, or vice versa; or it may be warm in the morning and cool before' night; such changes in temperature require changes in clothing. Particularly is this needful for the growing child. Prolonged chilling of the surface from exposure either to cold or to a draft of air is especially injurious.

"GIE ME MATCH BACK."

Robbie met a neighbour who was smoking some fine tobacco sent him by his son in America. So Robbie took out his own pipe. "Hae ye a match, Sandy?" he inquired. The match was forthcoming, but nothing more.

"I do believe," said Robbie: shyly, "I hae left my tobacco at home."

"'Then," said 'Sandy,' after a silenre, "ye might gie me match back again !"

FOR DADDA AT THE WAR. By Eusrx WILKINSON (Churchtown). Mamma, the hour for bed has come, The little stars are peeping; ' Dear mamma, hear me say my prayer— Oh, tell me why you're weeping? I hear you sigh,l see you look At dadda's empty chair; When evening comes we lonely feel, Because he is not there.

Dear mamma, pray with me to-night, And God will calm our fears; I'll fold my arms around your neck, And kiss away your tears. We know that dad will pray for us When evening shadows fall; Though in the midst of war's alarms, Daily on Him he'll call.

The moon is looking calmly down, Beneath her mellow ray. Together, by dad's empty chair, Dear mamma, let us pray For other weeping mammas, too. We know that Jesus cares For all the little children, And the hornet, with empty chairs.

PATRIOTIC VAGRANTS.

Five Out Of Six Respond To Country's Call.

GUARDIANS AND BABY'S NAME.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians, held under the chairman- ship of Mr. A. J; Jolley on Wednesday morning, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell said he wondered whether it would not be better to refer .back for further consideration the question of obtain- ing the half-yearly tenders, seeing that they now had "this terrible Budget to enlighten them." The Chairman advised him to give notice of his intention to submit a motion on the subjejct, and the reverend gentleman said he would do so.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
NAMING THE BABY.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said that, outside the Boardroom, there had been some discussion con- cerning the name to be given to the deserted baby girl, who had come into the hands of the Guardians. Some people thought that the name chosen by • the Guardians at the last meeting was not the most desirable cognomen that might have been found.

The Chairman: The matter has been settled. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: The name given to a child is very important, and I think the name in this case might be open to some little ques- tion. . .

Mr. G. Mather: Has the child been registered in the name which we gave her? The Master (Mr. W. -Loxham): The child has not been registered from here. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Then, if the child has not been registered, I would suggest that we give it a better name, and not send it into the world with a name like "Dorothy English," which, whilst it sounds very pretty, is hardly a practical sort of name, so to speak. It so hap- pens that the day on which the name was given was one of the Saints' days in connection with the Virgin Mary—the birthday of the Virgin, in fact—and therefore I do not think we could give the little girl a better Christian name than Mary. As to a surname—well, the child was brought to Warrington! ,So what is the matter with Mary-Warrington for a name? I think it is preferable to Dorothy English. and I am pre- pared to move that the name be changed to Mary Warrington. The Chairman said the matter was settled at ,the last meeting, and could not be altered that day. , Mr. W. C. Brooks suggested. amid laughter, that a still better name would be Mary Burton- wood—" the place Mr. Mitchell comes from " instead of Mary Warrington. THE REASON WHY!

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell asked whether it was true. that . casuals were not allowed .to attend divine service in the Workhouse chapel on Sun- day mornings. It was the general rule through- out the country that the vagrants should attend service on Sunday mornings, but there were ex- ceptions; and he would like to know whether the Warrington Workhouse was amongst such exceptions. The Master replied that all casuals were dis- charged from the Workhouse at seven o'cleck on Sunday mornings. There were none in the house during service time. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Supposing the casuals did not leave, would they be allowed to attend the service? The Master: If they requested to be allowed to attend the service we should grant them The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) thought the matter was one which required careful discus- sion. It must be remembered that the vagrants would only come in on the Saturday night, and that if they were allowed to go into the body of the house, when the other inmates were present, there was a risk that they might introduce in- fection. The Chairman advised the Rev. A. M. Mitchell to bring the matter before the House Com- mittee; that was the proper place to have it settled.

FROM CHILDREN'S HOME TO FORE DECK. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell referred to the fact that one of the boys brought up in ,the Padgate Homes joined the Cunard line of steamships upon leaving, but he had now gone into the Royal Navy. (Hear, hear.) In a letter "home" he made reference to the "very nice hot din- ners" he had at the homes. He (the reverend gentleman) mentioned that fact because it was not .often that the Local Government Board got much credit for what it did. (Laughter.) The Chairman Why should the credit be given to the Local Government Board? The Clerk: Yes, why? The Guardians fixed the dietary with which this young man was apparently so well pleased. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: .Ah but the Local Government Board approved it. (Renewed laughter.) • Miss nttiannt mentioned that letters bad been received from ether "old boys" of the Padgate •Homes, who were serving King and country, one in France an.a the other in the Dardanelles.

A QUESTION AND A RETORT.

Mr. G. Mather asked the Clerk to furnish a return showing the number of children in the Padgate Cottage Homes, together with a list of the officers engaged there, and the amount of wages which they received. Mr. Shaw: That's all very nice. The speaker went on to add that if Mr. Mather had attended the meetings at the Padgate Homes he would know all he wanted to know without coming before the Board and troubling the Clerk for information.

Some time later, just as the Board Were about to rise, 'Mr. Shaw said that he felt inclined to move that the information sought by Mr. Mather be not given. RECRUITING AMONGST THE CASUALS. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell asked how recruiting was progressing ,amongst the casuals who made use of the Workhouse, and added: "I think we ought to be finding some recruits from that very valuable source of strength to the Army." The Vice-Chairman (Mr. W. Furness) said that he had paid several visits to the casual wards with a view to inducing the occupants to offer their services. On one occasion all the men of suitable age were cortgregated together in one of the large rooms, and he had the opportunity of speaking to them collectively, as well as of talking to some of them individually. He was not successful hi getting any recruits, but, so far as he could, he made it unpleasant for the men who were of suitable age, and yet neither volunteered for the Army, nor for work on munitions. He. and the Master, who accom- panied him, made the casuals realise as far as they could that to be coining to that Workhouse in the manner they had done, and at a time like the present, was little short of an offence against the Realm. He added that if those who could not join had any desire at all to work, it would be found them. Several other times he had been to the house, but on most occasions the men were so diversified in character and age that he could not speak to them as a whole. _ _

Miss Broadbent: Have any recruits been got? The Vice-Chairman c Not directly. My general experience of recruiting is that you never know the result of your appeal at the time. It's " like bread thrown upon the waters. which returns after many days." The mind of the man who listens to the call is upset; he begins to feel uncomfortable, and, in many cases, some days afterwards, goes, to the recruit- ing office. That is the • experience of the recruiter. •

The Master: One morning we had six casuals of military age in the casual wards. One of them expressed his willingness to join, so I sent for the recruiting agent and he got five of the half-dozen to join up. (Hear, hear.)

" BARNACEE " SALE.

Raising Funds For The!wail

Military Home.

Glorious weather favoured Saturday after- noon's sale of work, &c.—held at Baruaere," Latchford Without (the residence of Mr. and Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes), in aid of the Thelwall Heys Military Conval6scent Home. Amongst others there were present: Mr. and Mrs. Jack- son Holmes, Mrs. James Fairclough, Mrs. Frost, Mrs. Densham, Mrs. Wheeler, Mr. and Mrs. Dimmelow, Mrs. Tickell, Mrs. Milling, Mrs. Wm. Tinslely, Miss Muriel Tinsley and the Misses Tinsloy (Penketh), Mrs. and the Misses Bracecamp, Miss Norton, Miss Eyre, Miss Bow- den, and many wounded and convalescent soldiers,

===== 0002601_19150925_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
DEEDS, NOT WORDS.

Mr. Holmes, who presided over the opening, said the cause of the wounded soldiers was ae much in the hearts of everyone in this country that that facit itself must be a sufficient appeal to all present to patronise the effort right royally, and show that they appreciated the work of the ladies who had promoted it. (Hear, hear.) Mrs. Fairclough, in declaring the function open, reminded those present that nowadays wherever they went they saw placards inviting them all to do their "bit." She thought that that day their "bit" was to empty the stalls, and make the sale a great success—riot only for the sick in the hospital, which would benefit, but also for the sake of Mrs. Jackson Holmes and her helpers, who had worked so hard. That day it was a case of deeds, not words, so she would keep them no longer, but declared the sale open, with the hope that they would gladden the hearts of Mrs. Holmes and the members of the Finance Committee of the Thelwall Con- valescent Home. (Applause.)
