===== 0002601_19150925_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER .25, 1915.

STUPENDOUS FIGURES.

Mr. MCKFttiNA'S first Budget was a tre- mendous affair, to be handled by a 'prentice hand, but he came. through the ordeal, with flying colours—as he was expected to do, after the masterly way he managed his recent record war loan. Of course, the -amount of revenue raised by taxation, existing and new, is only as,it were a drop in the bucket in com- parison with the nation's expenditure. The estimated revenue for the current year is £305,014,000, the estimated expenditure £1,590,000,000. Last year the realised deficit was £334,000,000. The CHANCELLOR of the EXCHEQUER puts the total of our dead- weight debt at £2,200,000,000, not allowing for the possible repayment of some or all of the advances made to our Allies. The effort to meet the burden which Mr. McKENNA has made has been admitted to be equitable. That is all that can be asked in these abnormal times. We shall have to pay, and our only concern is that the payment Shall be spread out as fairly as possible. We have new, strange and unwanted taxes intended to make our growing debt as small as possible; and that there has been a gener- ally expressed willingness to meet them proves that the heart of the country is sound. In Germany no new taxes have been imposed Means° the country cannot stand them. Our enemies are living upon hope. They hope to win and then make us pay. Those are assets that will not mature, and when the crash comes it will come with emphasis. For our part, we are not only able, but willing, to meet our liabilities to a considerable extent. We, too, have our hope for the future, but it is backed up by making practical provisions for the morrow, not the hope tliiat rests on the precarious practice of Counting one's chickens before they are hatched.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
DOCTORS AND WAR DUTIES.

We have received a circular signed by a score or so of distinguished medical men prac- tising in various parts of the country, and forming the Medical War Service Emergency Committee, asking for the syrripatbetic assist- ance of the public in the setting free of medical practitioners for service with the forcers. As we know, medical men have been amongst the most patriotic of our citizens, although patriotism in their case carries special risks. It is in the minimising of these risks that our correspondents ask the co- operation of the public. Every medical man who leaves his practice either appoints a locum tenens or arranges with a neighbouring doctor to attend to his patients during his absence. What is asked is that patients will make it clear in all cases that they intend to place themselves under the care of their own doctor when he returns. Insured persons are asked not to apply for transfer, but remain loyal to their own doctor until his retura, although they benefit in the meantime by the temporary service at their disposal. Medical mon remaining at home will do all they can not only to give the necessary service, but to aid their temporary patients to be loyal to their regular doctors. Another way in which the public can assist is by being as sparing as they can be in seek- ing medical aid. In ordinary times it may be excusable and advisable to call in the doctor on the slightest provocation, but these are the times for the display of our spartan quali- ties. Already the town has lost the services, for military reasons, of Drs. Smith, Suther- land and Ogilvie, and Dr. Law's position, as house surgeon at the jnfirmary, has been per- manently difficult to fill. When the Lord Derby War Hospital at Winwick has received its full complement of patients, and the War- rington practitioners are largely engaged there, as they have undertaken to be, the local shortage of doctors will be. pronounced, and those peopte with minor ailments must not be exacting in their claims. We must economise in doctors, as we must economise in gold!

===== 0002601_19150925_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
MEDICAL INSPECTION COIMAITTEE REVOLT.

The Education Committee on Monday night by all but a unanimous vote—the Mayor, Councillor Dickenson and Councillor Archer being the only opponents--Clecided to place the dispute which has arisen oet,wee.a

the Health Committee and themselves before tho Board of 14.1ducation. With a view to pre- venting undue publicity, wo presume, the committee discussed the matter in General Purposes Committee, but owing to the action of the Mayor, who objected to what load been don.e in committee, a very fair indication was given in open meeting of what had happened. The cool assertion was made that it was the fault of the Medical Inspection Sub-committee

that a compromise had not been arranged with the Health Committee. The Inspection Com- mittee have,more than once made it clear that they were prepared to accept a joint com- mittee of themselves and the Health Com- mittee in the appointment of officials whose work lies entirely, or almost entirely, in the schools. The offers having gone unheeded, even Dr. Joseph gives his committee up for a bad job. If it is true that the Health Com- mittee are willing to allow the Education Committee some voice in the appointment of their own officials, it is still open for them to say so. Hitherto they have stone-walled the suggestion, and the help of the Board of Education is now sought in the work of break- ing down what is an absurd barrier to the application of commonsense.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL SOCIETY PARADOX.

Such is the curious irony of circumstances incident to the war, that the Warrington Alusical Society finds itself better off in de- voting its concert-promoting efforts to local charities than in working for its own benefit. At the annual meeting of members, held on Tuesday, the hon. treasurer, Mr. John P. Reynolds, was able to announce that, while the society had not been able to pull through last season without a loss affecting its own funds of some £1.6, it had to set against this the earning of nearly £lOO, which had been divided amongst several of the town's War Funds—surely a highly creditable achieve- ment. Ordinarily, and when catering for the musical public on its own account, the Musical Society is fortunate if the three concerts of its season do not bring in their train a loss of £4O or £5O per annum. It was a happy idea therefore to place the society's activities as the management did twelve months ago, at the disposal of the Working Men's Infirmary Committee, to be exploited by them for the benefit of worthy objects, and the full measure of public support of the concert given in the early part of this year showed that unselfish effort of this kind is appreciated, as it deserves to be. Nor was the society's ex- perience of " The Elijah " concert in the Parish Church last December laCking in similar suggestions of a new and wiser policy which it may be well for the Musical Society to pursue during the stressful period of the war. A further concert in co-operation with the Infirmary Working Men's Committee is, we are pleased to notiee, contemplated for November next. Let us hope it will prove a great success.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
VISIT OF SIR ARTHUR AND LIM CROSFIELD.

COMFORTS FOR "TOMMY."

Liberal Women's Efforts To Raise Funds.

Under the auspices of the War Sewing Class in connection with the Warrington Women's Liberal Association, three ward tea meetings and concerts are to be held during the week com- mencing October 4th. The object of the func- tions is to swell the funds of the Sewing Class, which has already been the means of sending out huge consignments of much-needed comforts to the men in the trenches. With the approach of autumn, which, of course, presages another winter, it becomes essential that the efforts put forward by the women of Britain last winter should if possible, be redoubled, and Mrs. Edmund Osborne (the hon. secretary) and the ward captains and auxiliaries associated with her feel sure that the Liberal women of War- rington will fully maintain the excellent record of service which they have won for themselves in the past months. The first of the trio of social gatherings, all of which will be held in the Crosfield Memorial Hall, is fixed for Mon- day, October 4th. It will be held in connection with the Whitecross Ward and, as at each the othqr two socials arranged, Lady Crosfield will preside, and will be accompanied by Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart., and Miss Truda Cros- field. On the Tuesday night the Fairfield Ward will have their turn, and on the Thursday even- ing St. John's Ward. Sn interesting com- petition has been organised in connection with the events, a prize being offered for the best- knitted pair of soldiers' socks, the wool to be provided by the War Sewing Class.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
BLUE COAT SCHOOL.

New Master And Matron

Appointed.

On Thursday afternoon, at a meeting of the trustee S of the Warrington Blue Coat School, the appointment was made of Mr. and Mrs. Charles Ferris Weston .(of The School House, Thorndon, Eye, Suffolk) to the position of master and matron respectively—in succession to Mr. and Mrs. Edward itose, who recently expressed a wish to retire after 33 years' service. Mr. Weston—who was educated at the Colston School, Bristol, and Culham College, Oxford— has had a long experience as headmaster of public elementary schools, and since 1904 has been situatefl in that capacity at the Thorndon Church of England School. A first-class trained certificated teacher, he holds many art and science diplomas. - Mrs. Weston is also experienced in the teach- ing profession. Educated at the Girls' Endowed School, Ipswich, and the Stowmarket Secondary School, she was up to 1910 headmistress of the Brayesworth Junior •School. Of institutional work she gained her first knowledge from the fact of her being the daughter of the superin- tendent and matron of the Kerrison Reforma- tory School. From Thorndon Mr. and Mrs. Weston come with excellent testimonials from Sir Thomas H. Tacon, Mayor of Eye; Mr. G. J. Cripps, of Stenwell Lodge, Thorndon; Colonel Williams, of Eye; and the Rev. 11. A. Harris, of Thorn- don. They will enter upon their new duties very shortly.

A CABLE CO WEDDING.

NASH-HILL.

Two members of the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co.'s staff entered into the nuptial con- tract on Wednesday, in the persons of Mr. Harold R. Nash, the chief of the cable order. and estimating office, and Miss Florence Hilda Hill, confidential clerk to the manager and the secretary.

The ceremony took place at the Parish Church. Lowton, in the presence of a large number of friends and relatives.

The bride wore a dress of white crepe-de- ehene. with pearl trimmings and orange blossom, and was given away by her father, the bridesmaid being Miss Dora Prescott• niece of the bride.

The happy pair received numerous useful presents, and the reception was held at the bride's home, Heath-lane, Lowton.

The honeymoon is being spent at Aberystwith. Before the ceremony the staff of the Cable Works presented them with a service of cutlery, and an enamelled carriage clock to Miss Hill, in recognition of her twelve years' service with the firm.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 25, 1915.

THE MAYOR-ELECT.

Alderman Peter Peacock Again

Selected.

As was generally expected, the special meet- ing of the Town Council held on Monday for the purpose of selecting next year's Mayor, unanimously decided to ask the present occu- pant of the chair, Alderman Peter Peacock, to accept the position for a third year. Alderman Bennett was -the mover. and Alderman Tinnion the seconder of the proposal to this effect, and those two gentlemen, along with the Deputy- Mayor (Dr. Joseph), were appointed to convey the invitation to Alderman Peacock.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS WORSHIP ACCEPTS.

The deputation appointed at the meeting on Monday consisted of the .Deputy-Mayor (Dr. Joseph) and Aldermen Tinnion and Bennett. They duly waited on Alderman Peacock at the Town Hall on Friday afternoon and conveyed to him the decision of the Council.

• His Worship expressed his willingness to accept office for the ensuing year.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY GYMKHANA.

Warrington's Part In Forth- coming Big Rally.

DAY OF SPORTS AND MUSIC.

An attractive military event has been arranged for Saturday next, October 2nd, in the shape of a gymkhana and sports, which will be held on the Warrington Football Ground, Wil- derspool-road. The event will form part of a rally which is being undertaken in all the big towns throughout the country on the same day —by order of the War Office—the primary idea, of course, being to attract young men who still hold aloof into the ranks, by holding out to them an attractive picture of what may be dubbed the athletic•arid sporting side of mili- tary life. . , - •

- The gymkhana and sports meeting will be under the patronage of the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock), the Right Hon. the Earl of Derby, Colonel J. A. Moggridge, Lieutenant-Colonel D. E. Mocatta, Colonel J. D. Fairclough, and officers of the Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment).

===== 0002601_19150925_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
ATTRACTIVE SPORTS PROGRAMME.

A most attractive programme has been arranged, and it will include an exhibition of physical exercises and bayonet fighting by mem- bers of the Bantams' Battalion, whilst one of the features of the sports will be an' open race for munition workers in the town. Other attrac- tive events will be open races for soldiers' chil- dren—boys and girls under 12 years of age—and a 220 yards fiat race open to local.. Boy Scouts and merabers of the Church Lads' Brigade and the local Cadet Corps. The other events will include a sack race, a dispatch race, a Victoria Cross race, tugs-of-war, a three-legged race, a kit-bag " biffing " contest, &c., not -forgetting a one mile military cham- pion ship flat race, all the competitors in which will start from the scratch mark.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY TATTOO IN THE PARK.

Two bands will add to the enjoyment of the visitors—the Bands of the 2nd South Lancs. and the 2nd/4th South Lancs. (Territorials). In the morning the bands will march round the town, and in the evening there will be a military dis- play and tattoo in the Bank Park. The arrangements are being carried out .by Colonels Moggridge and Mocatta, assisted by the local Terlitorial officers. Although Lord Derby has kindly consented to give his patronage to the event he regrets that -he will be unable to be present. Tradespeople and residents are requested to decorate their places of business and residences with flags, &c.

BIG CHURCH PARADE ON THE SUNDAY.

There will be a Church Paade outside the Town Hall on Sunday, October 3rd, at three p.m. The Rev. Canon Willis will deliver a short address. The two bands and the troops are expected to be present. The attendance of the Volunteer Defence Corps is also antici- pated, and the Mayor and members of the Corporation will be present in state. The church choirs, &c., are being invited.

TRADES COUNCIL AND RECRUITING.

CAPT. MATHAMS'S REPLY.

Invitation To Trade Unions To Co-operate.

LIFE AND DEATH ISSUE.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—According to the report in your issue of the 18th the explanation offered by Mr. Dukes of the refusal of the Executive Com: mittee of the Trades Council .to meet repre- sentatives of the Territorial Force Association is clearly based on a misconception of the character of the invitation, which made it clear that the meeting with the Trades Coun- cil was desired, not for the purpose of asking the Council to take definite action in the matter, but with a.view to consulting them as to the effect of recruiting on labour generally, and the value or otherwise of methods which had been or were being adopted to secure re- cruits. It is to be deeply regretted that short- ness of notice did not give the Executive Com- mittee time to consult the Council in session, for, as Mr. Dukes admits, the Recruiting Cam- paign "differed, and differed widely, from any- thing they,had hitherto been called upon to consider," and was, moreover, as Mr. Dukes admitted, a matter of "paramount im- portance to the workers," and, indeed, "a question of life and death." It was because .the Territorial Force Association regarded the question as vital from the standpoint of the working men that they made an attempt to get into touch with them through the Trades Council as being the organisation which is supposed to represent them as a body. Notwithstanding their rebuff from the Trades Council, the association regards the matter as of sufficient importance to warrant their ex- tending an invitation to the secretaries and chairmen of all trade unions in Warrington to inbet their representatives to discuss the ques- tion of recruiting on the lines suggested to the Trades • Council. The unions, through their representatives, will not be asked to take any action other than they themselves may sug- gest, but they will be questioned as to how far their interests are affected, in order that better methods may be devised and possible griev- ances redressed. If there is any union, such as Mr. Dukes suggests, which thinks no action should be taken in the matter of recruiting, it may instruct its representatives to refuse the invitation.

In conclusion, may I inform Mr. Dukes that the correspondence between us was ' published not with a view to discrediting the workmen, who, after all, form the backbone of the British Army to-day? If the, correspondence has brought discredit on anyone, it can only be on those who profess to speak for the work- ing mpn, but who find that a whole fortnight is insufficient time to consult their representa- tives on a matter which the secretary of the Trades' -Council admits to be vital ,to their interests.—l am, sir, yours faithfully,;,_

ROBERT M. MATHAMS, Captain.

Officer Commanding, k Administrative Centre, 4th South Lancashire Regiment, Warrington, 24th September, 1915.

OUR COLOSSAL WAR-TIME BUDGET.

Local Opinions on the Proposals to Raise the Money.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
TAXES ON IMPORTS AND WAR PROFITS.

We have been favoured with the following representative local opinions on Mr. McKenna's epoch-making War Budget :

===== 0002601_19150925_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD.

Graduation: "A Memorable Fact In

Our History."

Sir A. H. Crosfield, Bart., writes : You ask me for my views on the Budget. To say that the Budget xna4s onerous additions to the burden of taxation which will be borne by every class in the country is only to utter what is already from end to end of the country a familiar commonplace. The Budget certainly does this, and in ordinary times such enormous additions to our taxation would be highly unpopular, received with a great deal of grumbling, and with no less of real or alleged consternation. But how striking the contrast to-day. These taxes will be accepted by our people, not merely without a grimace, but, I had almost said, with cheerfulness.

To See the Job Through. And the reason is obvious to all of us. , So ready an assent by Parliament and by the country is but a measure of the deep-seated, steadfast resolve of our people to go through to the, end with that great work to which we have set our hands, and, as the phrase goes, to make a thorough job of it. Therefore, one and all of us stay-at-homes must feel, as we do feel, thankful to do any- thing we can, and to make any little sacrifice we are called, upon to make to help our own kith and kin who are making such vastly larger sacrifices at the Front. This world-war is• being waged with many objects in view, which include the Freedom of both the large and the small nations of Europe; and we are fighting no less for the sake of Old England, the Freedom of the people of these islands, the integrity and final security of our mighty far-flung Empire. And well our gallant heroes at the Front know the supreme and overwhelming impor- tance of final victory where such life and death issues are in the balance. Never before in history have men come forward in their millions, .offering, if need be, to sacrifice health, life, all, as the men of our generation have voluntarily come forward to fight for the security of hearth and home, and ler the honour of the old country. They are heroes, and have proved themselves so in struggle after struggle where they have shown a courage and endurance unsurpassed in history. We at home should indeed be unworthy of them if we did not, one and all, each in our own sphere and capacity, do our utmost to back them up. But they need not doubt our resolution, and the reception of the Budget is just on example of this. Graduated Taxation.

May I add a word on one or two other strik- ing features of the Budget before I close? As far back as the 'eighties I was accustomed to urge the justice, importance and pressing need fok graduation on a great scale in our system of taxation. That principle has made marvellous progress in the last decade; but who could have foreseen, twenty or thirty years ago, that it would fall to a Coalition Government to give most striking effect to its application? That is indeed . a memorable fact in our history. Another fact which we may 'regard with no less satisfaction is the evident effort which has been made to avoid, as far as may be, adding to the burdens of the poorest of the poor among our people.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
MIL W. PETER RYLANDS.

impori Unties As Protection Against

Germany.

Mr. W. Peter Rylands, Chairman of the Conservative Association, and head of Rylands Brothers, Ltd., says : I think the Chancellor of the Exchequer has made a courageous and successful attempt to spread the heavy burden fairly over all classes of the community. The innovation of Import Duties is of interest, and while conditions are so abnormal too much reliance cannot be placed upon their effect; the working of these Duties will doubt- less be watched with curiosity. No one will be so stupid as to suggest that these Import Duties are a concession to any particular political opinion, but are merely dictated by considerations of common-sense and the neces- sities of the moment. Naturally I shall be pleased if such Duties remain part of our fiscal system hereafter, particularly if directed to protect us from the commercial attack of Germany when she is striving for revenge for her military defeat, which we, confidently hope is in store for her.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR HE.NRY ROBERTS.

Thinks ‘Var Profits Should Have Been

More Heavily Taxed.

Councillor Henry Roberts, Chairman of the .pducation Committee and a director of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., writes : I cannot help feeling gratified that the man who for many months has been one of the best abused members of the Government should have proved himself so fully equal to the most stupendous task which any Chancellor of the Exchequer has ever had to face. . .

The ABudget proposals are in the main fair and reasonable. I am not so much surprised at what they include as astonished at what they leave out.

I greatly regret that War •Profits have not been much more heavily taxed. The exploita- tion of the needs of the Empire for personal gain is grossly immoral, and while Capital is fully entitled to a 'fair return—say 10 per cent.—after proper provision for repairs and depreciation, any further profits should be returned, to the National Purse, and so ter- minate what has been the greatest cause of Labour unrest, and is nothing else but plunder. Expediency in Fiscal Economics. The proposal to place heavy import duties on foreign-made motor cars and other luxuries will probably be. opposed by ultra Free Traders, but it must not be forgotten that it is of supreme importance that the balance against us in our foreign trade should be reduced to the lowest possible figure, and, further, that as most, if not all, of our motor manufacturers have turned their huge work- shops for the time being into 'munition fac- tories, it would be a poor return for their patriotism if, when the war is over, they were left with little of their original trade. Expe- diency must be the governing factor on this as on many other questions for the period of the war, and probably for a long time after, and it is not at all improbable that the whole question of our "Fiscal Economics" will in future be regulated from the Imperial rather than from the National standpoint. Will Drink Be Dealt With Later'? The omission for the present of any further impost upon alcOholic drinks suggests the pos- sibility of their inclusion later, as it i& per- fectly obvious that the additional taxes imposed/ upon beer and spirits, a short time back have had no effect whatever in reducing consumption, and, further, it is notorious that a large proportion of the increase in wages received during the past year has been spent in intoxicants.

Willing to Foot the Bill. The alterations in the amount and scope of the Income Tax are very equitable and seem likely to prove thoroughly acceptable, and the same may be said of the "Breakfast Table" taxes. Speaking generally, the country has accepted the Budget proposals with a determination to "foot the bill," and has thus furnished the world with a further example of the unconquerable spirit of the. British people,—ready for any sacrifice that may be necessary to secure the • permanent freedom of the smaller 'European. States, the cyniplete destruction of Germanic .•"Kultur," and, universal Peaces . •

===== 0002601_19150925_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. C. E. PARKER

On War Profits And Partial Protection.

Mr. Charles E. Parker, President of the Liberal Association and Managing Director of the Penketh Tanning Co., Ltd., writes It is a pleasure to feel able to join in the general congratulations which have been offered Mr. McKenna in connection with his Budget proposals. Ile had a most difficult and compli- cated task to face. By general consent it is agreed that he has done justice, as far as that is possible, between the different classes of the community. In regard to the increase in the taxes already in existence little need be said. Granted that it is wise and necessary to retain the two methods of collecting the charge on income, the income Tax and Super Tax are fairly adjusted. I do not, however, see that there is much good in making the division. Could not the same object, that is the procur- ing of revenue, be attained under a system of tax graduated from top to bottom? It could be so graduated that it would impose practically the same burden as now rests upon the different individuals by the imposition of the double form of tax. It would thus come under the same machinery for collection and save a tre- mendous amount of labour. Perhaps, however, there are difficulties which I do not foresee.

War Tax Right; Not Permanent. The tax on war profits is a new form of taxa- tion, and is right in principle, but it will only be of *service as long as the wax lasts, and con- not be reckoned as a permanent source of revenue. When it was first suggested it ap- peared as if only certain manufacturing firms were to be brought in; further consideration has happily shown that this would be,inequit- able, as many firms would have escaped who have benefited by the war. It now applies to all firms, whether manufacturers or not, with the one exception of the agricultural industry, assessed under Schedule B. Ido not see why in equity these should escape the War Profit Tax. There is no industry that has more generally benefited by, high prices than the farmers. Of course, it may be contended that it is all im- portant to get as much out of the land " as.is possible, that to tax surplus profits would dis- courage industry engaged on the land. But the same might be said in regard to any useful manufacturing industry. Man does not live by bread alone, and the other things necessary to his life are or equal importance .to .his food. It must be remembered that no farmer would pay on normal profits, but only on the extra profits. The tax on extra profits will practically amount to sixty per cent., because the half is taken on the gross profits and not on the amount, after deducting the usual Income Tax. :Sup- pose the extra profit in any ' business is ...tlOO, the owner will be left with £4O after the deduc- tions of Income Tai and Surplus Profit Tax. We now come to what Mr. McKenna some- what loosely .calls expenditure on imported luxuries. These are clocks, watches, musical ingtrhments, plate-glass, and hats. I will %deal with the question of motor cars and cycles later..

Taxes to Enrich Manufacturers. Mr. McKenna says: "Anyone who takes the trouble to consult the returns showing the amount of our imports for the first six months of the year . . . . will be able to form 4. very good reason why these articles have been chosen." Following Mr. McKenna's advice I have consulted the returns, not for the six months, but for the seyen months ending July 31st, 1915, and find a good reason"why these things should not be taxed. The following are the figures:— Net Imports For Seven Months Ending July 31st, 1315. 1911. 1915. • Clocks and parts ......251,966 143,284 Watches and parts ... 627,974 565,712 Hats, &c. Plate-glass Musical instruments 581,838 123,068

£1,056,772 .t 1,277,898 1,277,898

£778,874 decrease.

It will be seen that in every case the imports have diminished, and that the reduction amounts on the whole to 38 per cent. This is largely due to the stoppage of trade with Ger- many and Austria, but -renewed competition from these countries is not yet on the sky line. Presuming the demand to be the same, and the tax to be effective for Protection, the trades engaged in these things have to make up 38 per cent. of the previous imports, equal .to a value of £13,000 per annum. It is clear therefore from a Protectionist point of view these industries needed no further help from a tax to stimulate them. The effect will be to enrich these manufacturers at the expense of the community, and to an extent, and out of proportion to the small gain to the Treasury by the tax. This stimulation will draw into these industries labour in great demand to assist other industries Snore im- portant at the moment.. From every quarter hear of trade being impeded by shortage of labour. The question is why should the con- sumer pay for the enrichment of specially pro- tected manufacturers, and why should British labour be withdrawn from useful work and to some extent be wasted in doing what others are willing to do for us at less cost? A leading principle in British taxation has hitherto been that the whole of the money taken from the consumer has gone to the coffers of the State, and not into the pockets of individuals. To make such a glaring departure from a principle of taxation, which has held good in this country for seventy years. for the sake of putting a drop into the bucket to help to weight the exchanged is too weak to be even considered. And to tax such goods as hats. plate-glass. clocks and watches as luxuries, when many things distinctly luxurious have been omitted, is equally absurd.

The 'Foreign Motor Tax. It seems to me that the tax on motors, cycles; and parts stands on somewhat a different foot- ing. We know that the bulk of the firms pro- ducing cars have been put out of action by the necessities of the war. Instead of making motors they are making munitiolts. Here is a branch of industry put out of action,. and not of their own will. To allow foreign makers to get a hold of the English trade when our own manufacturers are unable to compete would be manifestly unfair. Herein is a strong reason why this trade should be Safeguarded. At the same time I think* that help could be given in a better why and at a 'better period. I dO not, however, so much object to this tax as to the others for the reason stated. A manufacturer should not be allowed to benefit at the expense of the community. At the same time manu- facturers should not be made to suffer loss through the exigencies of the war and under conditions which are the result of compulsion.

It may appear ungracious to enlarge upon a small point in a Budget that is on the whole conspicuous for its courage and fairness. But each proposal must stand on its merits, and this part is open to grave objections, and I trust what seems to me to be its demerits will be thoroughly exposed in Parliament.

Baby's Terrible Death.

At the Infirmary on Sunday evening Hannah Kelsall—the two years and nine months' old daughter of Joseph and Hannah Kelsall, news- agents, of 38, Queen-street—died after burns. At about 7.40 on that evening the mother left her sitting on a chair in the kitaien whilst she went to another newsagent's shop a little lower down to attend to the business for a few min- utes. During her absence the child must have been playing with some matches which were on a chest of drawers, for when the mother re- turned she found her sitting on the floor with her nightdress in flames. She took her to the Infirmary, but the little girl died shortly after- wards.

At an inquest conducted at the Police Station on Wednesday night by Coroner S. Brighouse, the verdict was "Accidental death."

Learning To Be Useful.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee, held on Monday evening, the School Management Sub-committee reported that a return showing the attendance of scholars from the public elementary schools at the special subjects centres during the year 1914-15 had been submitted to them, and it was noted with regard to cookery that 265 girls had received instruction in the third, 459 in the second, and 840 in the first course of instruction —1,564 in all, in respect di 1,414 of whom grant had" been claimed from the Board of Education; as to handicraft it was noted that 20 boys had attended from one school, in respect of 18 of whom grant had been claimed; and with regard to light woodwork it was seen that 516 boys from three schools had received instruction in this subject, in respect of 254 of whom grant had been claimed.

Sunday Scholars' United Service.

Some hundreds congregated on Sunday -after- noon in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church—when the Warrington Sunday School Union united service was held. The Rev. P. Tucson presided and spoke inspiringly upon "writing on the sand with Selfishness and upon the rock with the Love of Christ."—The Rev. T. 'A. Young, in giving the address, said that in Jesus' days boys were told to be like palm trees. The palm trees were upright; and where they were one found springs of pure water, so that the traveller, when he saw the palm tree in the distance, knew that he could obtain refresh- ment without becoming inebriate; and they were always the same. Then they were useful to everybody; they grew fruit; afforded shelter a,nd helped to make homes; made mats and thread (with the weaker fibres); and made food. And one-half of the uses of the palm tree had not yet been discovered. All boys and girls should be like the palm tree—upright, a tem- perance advocate, and always helping others.— Mr. F. Morrall, on behalf of the Suitday School -Union, heartily thanked the Chairman and speaker.—On behalf of the union funds a col- lection was taken.

Emmanuel Church Mothers' Social.

The commitee of the Mothers' Union in con- nection ' with the Emmanuel Church, Bold- street, brought off a very successful social gathering on Thursday evening of last week, in the Institute, Knutsford-road. There was a large attendance and a most happy time was spent, thanks to the kindness of the friends who contributed to the enjoyable programme arranged by the committee. The following rendered solos during the evening: The Misses Bate. Hughes. E. Tanner and M. Whitmore, and Messrs. W. Dobson. G: Ray and G. Lloyd. Mr. T. Hughes provided two humorous read- ings. A pianoforte solo was nicely played by little Mabel Whitely, and little Kathleen Bibby contributed a recitation. Miss E. Jones ably accompanied the soloists, with the assistance of Miss Elsie Landon. who accompanied Messrs. G. Ray and G. Lloyd. During an interval refreshments were handed round by the lady workers. The gathering, which was presided over by the Rev. W. T. Jaye, expressed its thanks to those who had so kindly provided the evening's entertainment. The mothers will meet every Monday evening at 7.45 D.M.. under the leadershin of Mrs. Gandy, who is hoping to see a good rally for the winter. "The Sin of Drunkenness."

Mr. W. Beswick gave the address at the last Pleasant Sunday Afternoon at Wycliffe Con- gregational church. He took as his subject " The Sin of Drunkenness." It was well, he said, to be temperate in all things, and espe- cially in the matter of intoxicating drinks. He referred to the curtailment of the opening hours of licensed premises, brought about as a result of the war, and said that grateful as Temperance people were for such restrictions its had been brought 'about, they would like to see , the drinking facilities still further cur- tailed. They could not shut their eyes to the fact that there was too much drinking going on among the soldiers, who, from various causes, were at home. Drinking on the part of the fighters could neither he for the good of the plucky fellows themselves, nor could it be for the good of the nation.—Mr. Ben Farrar (the secretary of the P.S.A. Brotherhood) was in the chair and he proposed a vote of sympathy with a fellow-member, Mr. G. Boyle, who has re- cently undergone an Operation. Another vote of sympathy was passed with Mr. J. Davies in his long affliction, and with Mr. Beardsell on the severe illness of his mother. Mr. Farrar also urged the members to turn up in goodly num- bers next Sunday in order to welcome the Rev. T. T. James home again. Mr. Cotton also offered a few remarks.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR COLOSSAL WAR-TIME BUDGET— continued.

the most favourable consideration to the pro- posals of the Chancellor. One's first feeling is that the Budget is a well-conceived and business-like proposal, and on further study this conclusion is not materially altered.

The increase in the Income Tax was ex- pected. It was originally a War Tax, and though it. is now assuming alarming propor- tions, I believe it will be willingly paid. The lowering of the limit of exemption to. £l3O is undoubtedly a wise provision and equally so is the etax on war profits.

New Taxes Insignificant. The new taxes seem only remarkable for their insignificance, and no one need think they have any bearing on the question of Tariff Reform. Tariff Reform and Free Trade as party cries are dead, as the ,state of things existing at the conclusion of the war will require a re-statement of political and economic beliefs. What at the present time is moil important is how this new taxation will effect the question of foreign exchange. The postal changes will excite the greatest amount of adverse comment, but in view of the needs of the country in this critical tithe they are probably necessary and are not likely to be permanent. What of the Civil Service? The great omission seems to be that there is no hint or suggestion of any reduction in the Civil Service expenditure. When .we re- member how the Government have preached economy to the nation, and how during late years by creating hosts of new officials they raised the expenditure by leaps' and bounds, it would have been consistent if they had taken some steps to curtail this vast and possibly unnecessary outlay./ Taking all the circumstances into considera- tion, I believe this. to be a wise and honest Budget, and that it., will meet with general approval.

Latchford Cadet Corps. By an oversight the part played by the Latch- ford Cadet Corps in the recent recruiting cam- paign went unacknowledged. They paraded each night in the week and rendered valuable service, which was much appreciated by the recruiting authorities.

Help for Prisoners of War.

The list a subscriptions this week shows a serious falling off. About £3O per week is re- quired to enable a parcel to be , sent once a fortnight to prisoners of the South Lancashire Regiment who have not been "adopted." This. worked out, represents about Is. 6d. per man per week. It will therefore be recognised that the extra fare of our men (many of whom are wounded) in Germany, is very meagre.

Curaey for Scholar-Athlete.

An old Botelarian and son of a gentleman well-known in local scholastic circles, Mr. Harry Hands, of Downing College, Cambridge, and the Theological College, Ely, was on Snn- day ordained by the Bishop of Southwark and has been appointed to the curacy of St. Paut'e Church, Newrington. Mr. Hands, who was born at Widnes, spent his early years at Hale- wood, subsequently coming to Warrington. where his father holds the appointment of assistant master at ,the People's College, and where he attended for some years, prior to pro- ceeding to the Boteler Grammar School, of which he subsequently became head boy, and where he won prizes for Greek, Roman and English history and for English literature. Both at the Grammar School and at the 'Varsity he distinguished himself in athletics, and whilst at Downing he gained his colours for Association football, and in his third year was made captain-secretary of his college club. He also won his colours for hockey and cricket, and was one of the tennis "six." He has frequently played cricket with the Warrington first team.

Travellers and Languages. At the quarterly meeting of the Warrington and District Branch of' the United Kingdom Commercial Travellers' Association, held at the Lion Hotel last Saturday, the question of the importance of travellers having a knowledge of foreign languages after the war was over, ivos discussed, and it was unanimously decided to form a French class. The Executive C,ommittee have secured the services of an experienced teacher, and several members gave in their names as wishing to join. Full particulars WI be sent to any other member on application to the Secretary, Mr. T. Percival, Glass Works, Bank Quay.

The -Exec- utive Council having expressed , desire for one of their members to visit eaeli branch during the coming winter, it was decided to ask Mr. Coysh to come to Warrington, en a date to be fixed by him., Mr. W. G. Hayes reported on the progress of the fund which has been opened to endow bed at the Piner Schools. He said he he_ ~Ped those members who have not yet subscribe° would do so as soon as possible, as 50 guineas oa of the 100 guineas required', had to be sent be- fore the end of the year. The object iS worthy one, and it is sincerely hoped a g°°" response will be made. An invitation from the Liverpool Branch to send delegates to the Conference Committe having been received, Messrs. H. Walsh and vv. G. Hayes were appointed.. lt was .decided to invest MO of the funds af, the branch in the War Loan. ea • The Chairman (Mr. IL Walsh), ,was,tho for presiding.

SERGEANT WINDELL.

said, done his duty and he was glad he had done it, and, of course, he was also glad that those in authority over him had thought what he did worthy of special recognition. There was, however, one thing he would like the newspaper men to mention if they would, and that was that in accomplishing the first of the two acts which won him the medal, he had the assistance of Lance-Corporal J. THE DEEDS WHICH WON THE MEDAL. The official records set forth that the D.C.M. has been conferred on Sergeant Windell for two acts of conspicuous gallantry. The first was performed near 11l es on October 21st, when, during an attack by the enemy, the Sergeant, (then a Corporal) assisted in with-- 41rawing.- a: gun. from a house 400 yards • in front of the British firingTline. At the time the Germans were only 150' yards away from the house. The second gallant act performed by the D.C.M. consisted in withdrawing a machine-gun whilst under heavy fire. "On both occasions," adds the official record, "he acted voluntarily.

The Sergeant is not a Northerner, but hails from London, his home being at 246, Devon. •shire-street, Mile End. Before being enrolled in the South Lancashires, he attempted enlist- ment in a mounted regiment; but his lack of inches was against him. ilowever, at-3 he him. .self put it, he is not sorry he got amongst the Lancashire lads, for they are fine fighters and tam "pals."( Before he joined up the Sergeant was engaged in the cabinet-making industry.,

===== 0002601_19150925_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COLONEL'S COMMENDATION.

The presentation ceremony was brief and to the point The various detacliments now undergoing training at the Barracks were drawn up to form three sides of a large square and to the right of them stood the battalion hand, who, prior to the arrival of Colonel Moggridge—who has command of the depot— and Colonel •Mocatta—the chief local recruit- ing officer—regaled the waiting " Tornmies" with a merry rag-time melody. When the two Colonels made their appearance on tile parade ground the men came smartly to attention, and at the word of command Sergeant Windell stepped forward from a rear rank and ad- vanced to Within a pace of the Commanding Officer, when he came smartly to attention. • Stepping forward, the Colonel, with a few words of high commendation, pinned the- little silver token on the breast of the waiting soldier and then shook hands with him and congratulated him upon his bravery and upon the fact that he had been spared to come home safely and receive his medal that morning. He trusted that the Sergeant's gallantry would be a source of inspiration to many another man in the British Army.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
GUEST OF THE MESS.

Merry Evening With The Depot Sergeants.

In honour of the presentation, 'Sergeant Windell was, on Tuesday evening, the guest at It smoking concert held in the Sergeants' Mess .at the Barracks. The chair was taken by Sergeant-Major Quick, who was supported by C.S.M. Nowall, Company Q.M.S. Stevens and Sergeant W. McDonald. There were many guests, including Captain and Q.M. Crane and several Sergeants, who have recently returned from the Front on fur, lough, as well as several ladies and civilian friends.

During the evening the Chairman submitted the toast of Colonel Moggridge, Captain Crane and the other Officers of the depot. Captain Crane, in responding, expressed the pleasure it had given him to spend the evening in the company of so brave aml true-hearted a soldier as Sergeant Winklell, D.C.M., whose 'health he asked the company to drink. The guest of the evening suitably expressed las thanks and added that he had been twelve months at the Front.

The toasts of "The ,Lads in thp Trenches" was heartily drunk. A first-rate programme was sustained by Miss Itoberts, Captain Crane, Dr. Hughes, the Chair- man, Sergeant-Instructor Tucker, Sergeants 'Williams, Bibby and Leadbetter, and Mr. T. Stout.

The proceedings. terminated , with the singing of "Auld Lang Syne," Mr. H. Ashton having 'first , briefly expressed the pleasure of the *civilians at being invited to be present.

NATIONAL RESERVIST'S DEATH.

Wounded 'Warriors Carry Him To The Grave.

The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Monday of Private Andrew Gilhooly, whose home was at 25, Winwick-road, and who was a member of No. 1 Supernumerary Co. of the 2/ 4th South -Lancs. Regiment. He passed away on the Thursday of the previous week' from heart disease.

In his 50th year, Private Gillooly joined the National Reserve seven Months ago, and lately he had been on guard duty in Surrey. For- raerly he was a member of the sth Royal Irish Lancers, with *horn he served seven years at home and five years in India. He was on duty as a Reservist in Ireland 'during the Boer War. lie leaves a widow and five children.

The funeral was marked by military honours, a number of wounded members of the Warring- ton Territorial Battalion acting as bearers, whilst others followed the coffin to the Ceme- tery, where the usual volleys were fired over the grave and the "Last Post" was sounded by the buglers. Among the wreaths was one from the Hay- dock-street Tannery Co., for whom the deceased had worked prior to joining the National Reserve.

BROTHER TO BROTHER.

Composed by Gunner H. Jarvis, R.F.A,, a patient in' the WhitecrOss Hospital, in memory of his brother, Private Wm. Jarvis, sth Man- chester 'Regiment, killed in action at the Dardanelles on sth August, 1915.

THE HERO. We heard the news,: we bore the strain, His death has caused us grief and pain; But well we know we'll meet again.

He died a Hero. He left us all to go and fight For Home and Freedom, "Right" not "Might, His task well done, he fought the fight

And died a Hero. But now his fighting and troubles are o'er, And he is safe on that beautiful shore Where grief and pain, they, are no more. "Well Done, Brave Hero." So brother o' mine, sleep on and rest. For life was lent thee, and God knows best. • Farewell--good-bye, sleep on and rest. Dear Brother—" Good-bye."

KILLED.

SIR ARCHER CROFT.

Death Of A G'allant Soldier And True

Gentleman.

In last Saturday's " Examiper " we an- nounced that uncjfficial news had, been received that Sir Archer Croft, son-in-law of Mr. J. C. Parr, Grappenhall, had been killed in Gallipoli. In Monday's " Times" the baronet's name was one of several officers whose deaths appeared in that day's obituary column, but "whose names have not up to the pre4ent appeared in the official casualty lists." Captain Sir Herbert Archer Croft, Bart., Ist Herefordshire Regiment, of Lugwardine Court, Hereford, who was reported missing early last month, is now sta6d to have been killed in Gallipoli on August 11th. Born on September sth, 1868, he succeeded his father, Sir Herbert George Denham Croft, M.P. for Herefordshire, the ,ninth baronet, in 1902. Ire formerly held a commission in the 4th Shropshire Light Infantry. Sir Archer married first, in 1892, Kathleen (who died in 1898), second daughter of John Hare, of Invercargill, New Zealand, and secondly, in 1903, Katherine Agnes, 'eldest daughter of Mr. Joseph Charlton Parr, of Girappenhall Reyes, Warrington. He is suc- ceeded by his son, James Herbert, who was born in 1907. Confirmation Of The Sad News.

The most circumstantial account Of many we have seen, says the "Hereford Times," is that contained in a letter now in our possession written by Sergeant C. G. Corner (2036), B Com- pany, Ist Herefordshire Regiment—Sir Archer Croft's own company. The letter is undated, but the envelope bears the postmark August 27th, 1915. Sergeant Corner says— "Of course you have heard abour poor Capt. Sir Archer Croft being killed. He was my Captain. We had lost our regimen't, and he told me to lie down under cover while he went through some bushes to look and see if he coul find anyone, and me and another chap, his ser- vant in England, were to wait for him, but he never came back. But we advanced about a mile three days after and we found him near the Turkish lines. They picked him up and he died on the stretcher on the way to hospital."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Beloved In Hereford.

In his own village of Lugwardine the late Baronet was beloved for his many good quali- ties in public life. In his family life Sir Archer Croft was most happy. The county will moutn the passing of a public benefactor, a gallant soldier, and a true gentleman.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPL. G. E. BROOME.

Actinwlleachnaster Of Secondary School Loses His Brother.

News has reached England that Corporal G. E. Broome, of the 6th Manchester Territorials, was invalided home from the Dardanelles, but died on the hospital ship Delta on Saturday, September 11th, and now lies buried at sea, between Malta and Gibraltar, Corporal Broome—who would have attained his majority last Saturday—was the brother of Mr. J. S. Broome, acting-headmaster of the Secondary School, Already he had shown great promise in different spheres, particularly in the Manchester Tramways Offices, which he entered after completing four years' education at the Salford Secondary School. A particularly cap- able swimmer and also an enthusiastic cricketer and footballer, he took a prominent part in the Lads' Club connected with St. Luke's, Weaste —which church he attended. To the Terri- torials he had belonged for some time, and was called up at the outbreak of hostilities. When seized with illness he had seen three week's service at the Dardanelles.

Extremely popular with all he met, Corporal Broome will be missed in many circles.

PRIVATE JOSEPH CROSS.

"Died A Soldier's Death," Writes His

Officer.

"I regret to have to inform you of the death of your son, which occurred this morn- ing. An unlucky shell killed him instan- taneously. The same shell was the cause of several other casualties. It is with the greatest regret that we learn that the services of a good soldier have been lost to his country." In these words did Lieut.-Colonel W. E. Sellers, com- manding officer of the Bth Battalion of the King's Own Scottish Borderers convey to Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Cross, of 75, Orford-street, the news that their son, Private Joseph Cross, was killed in France on Tuesday,- September 7th. Struck By Shell Whilst In Billet.

On Monday of last week the following letter

was received from the Rev. Edward A. -Forbes, the Church of England Chaplain of the Regi- ment :

"Will you please let me write a few lines to you to say how intensely sorry I am for you? Your poor boy was struck by a shell while sitting in his billet. He cannot have suffered at all, as he died instantaneously. I know how deeply you must feel this, but it is a glorious thing to have given so, much for one's country. I pay God may bless and comfort you. The Colonel and I buried his poor body in a little cemetery, and the place will be marked with a cross with his name and regiment on it."

Private. Percy Regan also wrote expressing hi 4 sympathy, and added:—" His death is a great loss to 'me and the company, as he was well liked by all. He is buried in a nice grave not far from where he was killed."

He gave us 'no trouble what- ever, and had the esteem of all who knew him. As his officer I offer, on behalf of the company, our most heartfelt sympathy with you in your bitter loss. He died a soldier's death." ,Private Cross, who was eighteen years of age. received his education at the• St. James' School, Latchford. He "joined up" in August of last year, having been employed pre- viously as a machinist with his father in Heaton's Yard. , Private Cross was known as a quiet, well-behaved and hard-working youth, and his loss is felt not only by his parents but by a; largo csirele of friends and acquaintances.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
REPORTED DEAD.

Rifleman Who Survived Nine Wounds

BROTHERS IN CASUALTY LIST.

Two Warrington brothers who are serving .in the Rifle Brigade both had the misfortune- to be placed on the casualty list. Their name is Martin and their father lives at 9, Clifton- street, Latchford. . Went Through Battle of Hooge.

One of the twain, Rifleman Harry Martin, who is attached to the 9th Battalion of the Rifle Brigade, has recently been home on fur- lough, but has now gone back to his depot at Winchester. During an engagement near Ypres on June 22nd he loSt the sight of his right eye, and. received no fewer than eight wounds in the chest and legs. Six days before his mis- fortune befel him he took part in the historic fight at Hooge, in which the Lancashire men distinguished themselves so nobly. He de- scribed the battle as `f awful" and opined that it was without doubt one of the hottest of the whole war.

Shell Burst In A Little Group.

When he met with his multiple injuries he was leading a section of bomb-throwers, and suddenly 13. shell burst in the middle of the little group. killing several of his comrades outright. He was wounded shortly after eight o'clock at night, and by four o'clock the fol- lowing afternoon he was in hospital at Boulogne. Writing to his father on Tuesday of this week the gkillant Rifleman announces his safe arrival at his depot, and adds: "I don't know what they are going to do with me, but I have had a good time up to now. We do noth-, ing at all! I have come across some of my own men; in fact my Own sergeant is here. He was surprised to see me, as he had. re- ported me dead to the 'Captain, because I did not come back to the battalion after I was hit."

The Rifleman was educated at St. James' School, Latchford, and prior to joining the Army worked in the Corporation Gas depart- ment. He is a member of the Latchford Con- servative Club.

Wounded Brother Convalescent.

Rifleman Robert Martin, brother of the much-wounded man, has. been suffering from a severe wound .in, the, head, but is now re- ported as convalescent.. :Re is in hospital in. France:. A married man, his home is in • Slater- street, and.. before he joined, the . Army ,he worked als- .a 'moulder for the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd. •

• the right shoulder whilst taking 'part in the recent fighting ,in the vicinity ,of the Dar- danelles. He is now in a Bittallighani Hospital.

Enlisted At Same Time As Father.

Private Jackson, who is twenty years of age, made his home in peace. times with his parents at 4, Orchard-street. He enlisted shortly after the outbreak of war, at the same time as his father, PriVate Charles Jackson, who is engaged on home service as a member of the Loyal North Lancs. Regiment. • Previous to the outbreak of war, Private William Jackson was employed by Messrs. Rylands Bros. Ltd.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLEMAN T. BATES.

IVounded A Second Time, But Still

Cheery.

Rifleman T. Bates, whose home is with his parents—Mr. and Mrs. Charles Henry Bates—at 14, Bowes-street, off Winwick-road, has been wounded for the second time, and still, to use his own expressive phrase, is "not downhegrted." He conveyed the news to his father and mother in the following letter: "I am sorry to say I have been wounded again. I have got it in the head ,this time. The Wound is not so bad, but, of course, it is in a nasty place. I reckon I am all right for a couple of 'months now, an.l should like to get home on sick furlough, but I don't think the hurt is bad eivuoi ror that." The letter, -which was wrvti fTorn Vie Cana- dian Hospital at Le Treport, concludes with the observation: "Being in this hospital is like being in heaven, as compired iith the trenches. They look after us here all right !" Going On All Right n England.

Subsequently a short message was recei,,Kl from the wounded man announcing that his conjectures had proved false, and that be hail arrived in England and had been drafted to a

Dardanelles in July, is a patient in the hospital which has been fitted up in the ballroom of Torquay Town Hall, suffering from a wound in the left arm and the .after effects of dysentery. He is 33 years of age, and lives with his wife at Haydock-street. . It, was during a visit of Mrs. Williamson to her husband a fortnight ago that their Majesties the King and Queen paid visit to the hospital.. When his Majesty began his tour of the. beds it happened that Private Williamson's was the

first In the ward, and he had the honour of receiving the King's first inquiries. Noticing the injured arm, his Majesty asked him how he came by his wounds, and on being told' that they were received in the Dardpelles, his Majesty remarked, "1 snppose you had it pretty hot there?" "Yes, your Majesty," replied the gallant Warringtemian; " but we made it hotter for them," a display of spirit the King seemed to appreciate. Her Majesty also taade a tarn of the hospital, and subsequently spoke a few kindly words to each patient. There were two other Warrington men in the hospital at the time—Private James W. Skel- horn, 16, Ashton-street, of the Territorials, whose record and portrait appeared in "The Examiner," July 24; and Private Jas. Cullen, 13, Cyril-street, 6th South Lancashires, whose record' and portrait appeared in our issue of September 4th. - Mrs. Williamson tells us that her husband and the other patients in this Torquay hospital report that the King himself could not be better attended to than they, are. Private Williamson is on the Liberal Workers' Roll of Honour. His nephew,- Ernest William- son, was killed in France as stated in our columns.

PRIVATE AV. HENNESSY.

Turkish Sniper Parted His Hair While

Fetching Water.

Private William Hennessy, son of Mr. Daniel Hennessy, 1, Shepherd's-row, Orford-street, enlisted in the Loyal North Lancashire Regi- ment last August, and is now, in hospital at Alexandria, having been wounded in the head in Gallipoli. Writing to his sister, Mrs. Jane Smith, 17, Orford-street, Private Hennessy says he is "in the pink except for the wound, which I am sorry to tell you is pretty bad. . . . I was fetching water when a sniper parted my hair. You should see the Turks run; some of them could beat Postle. .. . They have started to whitewash their bullets, so we can see them coming."

In a-letter to his parents he praises his treat-

Wounded

ment in hospital, and hopes to be "round Fletcher's in a month."

Private Hennessy, who is nineteen years of age, was a scholar at St. Mary's, Buttermarket- street, and was up to last August a range-fitter at Messrs. Fletcher Russell's Works.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE SAMUEL BALMER.

IVlliteeross Fusilier Soon Rack In

Whilst serving with the Bth Lancashire Fusiliers at the Dardanelles, Private Samuel Balmer, of 2, Pott's-yard, off Church-street, Warrington, received a shrapnel wound in the right shoulder. ' In word home dated August 25th he 'says his casualty occurred during a big charge on the enemy's trenches four days previously. "I have had two pieces of lead taken out of my shoulder," he says; "I think the wound will soon heal up, and I will be back in the firing line. Please sent me a box of Woodbines; we can't buy any over here, and we are dying for a smoke.'" The son of Mr. T. Balmer now of 18, Mimer- street, Private Balmer was born at Dunham Massey, but came to Warrington as a boy, and since leaving school has worked for the White- cross Co. He is married and has one little girl. The Lancashire Fusiliers he 'joined ten days after the outbreak of the war. - -

In a further postcard received by his wife two or three days ago; he says he is still going on well, though not having been sent to the 'base. He repeats that he hopes shortly to be again in the firing line.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE W. MONKS.

Old Botelerian Recovering From Wound In The Head'.

Private William Monks, of the firm of Messis. Wm. Monks and Sons, grocers, Bridge. street, has been woundea while serving in France with his regiment, the Inniskilling Dragoons, attached to the Bengal Lancers. The wound is in the, head, and he now lies in the Second Canadian Hospital, France, where he is progressing favourably. The wounded man, who is the son of Mr. William Monks, of Moore, Cheshire, received his education at the Boteler Grammar School, Warrington'. It was in August of last year that he responded to the very urgent call which was made to the young men of the country. He has been in Francesince April.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN CARROLL.

Writes That His Hurts Are "Only •A

Few Scratches."

Official news has been received by Mr. and Mrs. John Joseph Carroll, of 71, Howley-lane, to the effect that their son, Private John Carroll, of the Royal Warwickshire Regiment was wounded whilst serving with the Mediter, ranean Expeditionary Force on July 27th. ,Describing, in a letter to his parents, the nature of his Founds, Private Carroll says they are "only a few scratches on ray arm and chest." He also mentions that Nicholas, the well-known Warrington half-back, is serving with a Welsh Regiment in the Mediterranean Expeditionary Force. Private Carroll is now in a convalescent camp making a first rate recovery. Keen Footballer Who 'Listed Early On.

Private Cazroll, who is twenty years of age, attended as a boy, the National School. Prior to the outbreak of war he was employed by the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd. He is a keen footballer, having played for the Latchford Athletic Football Club. He wor- shipped at St. Clement's Mission Church. He enlisted on September Ist a last year.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN BARRY.

Crosiield's Employe Hit In Dardanelles

-Fighting.

Whilst serving with the 9th Battalion of the Lancashire Fusiliers in the region of the Dar- danelles, Private John Barry was wounded on August 21st. So stated the official communi- cation which was a few days ago received by his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Barry, who live at 32, Parr-street. Prior to is enlistment, Private Barry, who is twenty-seven years of age, was employed by Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons Ltd., and had •previously worked for the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd.

Well-known in Piscatorial Circles,

He is a v.-Al-known local angler; as also .is

his father. He takes a very keen interest in football. having played for both Richmonds and Crosfield's works teams. Whilst undergoing his training he has also indulged in his favourite outdoor recreation. His brother, Thomas, is training with the Warrington Territorials in Surrey.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
PRISONERS OF WAR.

Rifleman Hayes In Communica.

tion With His Friends.

Rifleman Thomas Hayes, 2nd Battalion Rifle Brigade, son of Mr. Thomas- Hayes, tailor, 3,. Sparling-street, and a prisoner of v-ar at the Rinnbahn Camp, Munster, Westphalo, Ger- many, is in regular communication with his relatives in Warrington. He was employed by Messrs. Armitage and Rigby, but enlisted in the second month of the war, proceeding to the Front early in the present year.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
Neuve Chapelle Victory.

He was at the battle of Neuve Chapelle irt March, when the British Forces made a great dent in the German lines.

The following is an extract from orders dated 14th March, 1915, by Lieut.-Creneral , which the gallant Rifleman sent home:— - " The brilliant success which the troops of the Corps have achieved in the capture of Neuve Chapelle is of the first importance to the Allied cause, especially at this period of the war. The heroism and gallantry of the, regimental officers and men and the assistance afforded them by the artillery units is deserving of the 'highest praise, and the Corps Com- mander desires to congratulate them on the severe defeat they have inflicted on the enemy, whose losses amount to not less than 4,000 mem in killed and prisoners alone. The magnificent behaviour of the infantry units is deserving of the highest commendation, and in deploring the loss of those gallant comrades who have given their lives for their King and country, hopes that all officers and men fully

realise that what they have accomplished, in breaking thiough the German line, is an achievement of which they should all feet justly proud."

Other Missing Warringtonians. Later on, May 9th, he was captured by the Germans in an engagement, and in three weeks a letter was received from him by his friends, who now hear from him every fortnight. At the time he was captured several other War- ringtonians belonging to the Rifle Brigade were wounded, captured, or reported missing. Rifleman Starkey, who subsequently was at Raddon Court Hospital, and Rifleman Lunt, who has been home on furlough, were wounded. His brother-in-law, Mr. Hands, at the Infirmary, wrote to him asking whether he knew anything of Rifleman Simister and Rifleman Kinsley, who are missing, but he has heard nothing of them. Rifleman Kent, who recently died of wounds at Blackpool and was buried in Warrington Ceme- tery, was a comrade-in-arms of Rifleman Hayes, and was the first to send word that the latter was missing.

Rifleman Hayes, who celebrated his birthday in the trenches, is a St. James' School old boy, and is " adopted " by Mr. -,Stansfiel& the head master,. who will forward him periodically parcels of comforts subscribed for by the scholars of the school. In his letters home he has asked that cigarettes and butter be not sent, as plenty are obtainable, In the early days of his captivity he asked for bread to be sent, but the treatment of prisoners , appears to have improved and that request has not been made lately. His latest letters state that he is in the best of health, and wishes to be remembered to all his friends. 'Rifleman Hayes formerly worked for Messrs. Robertson, Latchford, and three seasons ago played for that firm in the final in the Associa- tion Works Competition.

LANCE-CORPORAL T. BENION.

Former xt yeliffe Scholar Missing In

Dardanelles.

Lance-Corporal Thomas Benion, of the 6th South Lancashires, Kitchener's Army, is officially reported missing. On Sunday morning news was received by his wife at 7, Smith-street, Lower Walton, in word from the War Office. No further news is yet to hand. - Twenty-five years of age, the Lance-Corporal is a native of Warrington, and received his education at the Wycliffe Schools. He also has been a regular attender at the Wycliffe Sunday Scthool and Church—of which he is a member.

Missing,

First employed at the Wire Ropery, he after- wards transferred to the Firth Company—with whom he had for five years been a wireworker when hostilities broke out. One of the earliest to answer the call, be enlisted that same month and at the Dardanelles has seen, some three, months' service—during which he has written home upon different occasions in cheery style. Be is an enthuiiastic 'follower of the Wilde. 1)001 football matches, •

More Wounded Depart.—On Tuesday morning four wounded soldiers left the Red Cross Hos- pital—in which there are now 20 patients.

Local Contractor's Tender Accepted.—At the monthly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, held on Tuesday, the tender of Mr. Holt, contractor; &c., of Lymm, for the repair- ing of the Crowley-lane Bridge, Crowley, was accepted.

Lance-Corporal J. H. Booth.—ln hospital at Rouen, Lance-Corporal James 11. Booth—of the Ist Welsh Regiment—continues to make good progress after his admittance through sickness 'on August 21st. It will be remembered that only a few weeks' ago ho visited home, bringing with him (among other war relies) a revolver which he had from a German officer.

An Honest Girl—A rortnight ago' a one pound note was picked up on 62. Statham road by a fifteen-year-old girl named Lily Ward, and she at once took it to Councillor F. Lockyer. Shortly afterwards, and with much joy, a little girl claimed it—the owner being Mrs. RaYens- croft, of Newficldg. The youthful finder's esample is one which may well be followed.

Former Statham Lady's Poems.—Mrs. M. A. gar4selmeyer, of Hale-road, Altrincham—f or- merly of Ivy Cottage, Statham—has written two short and touching poem's, "A Mother To Her Soldier Son," and "A Soldier's Return From The Front To His Mother," which have been disposed of in some hundreds in aid of the Red Cross funds. Her Majesty, Queen Alexandra, has graciously accepted copies of the poems.

Canadian Soldier-Visitor.—To relations at Bridle-road, Lymm, a visit has just been paid by Private R. A. Burdett, of Princess Patricia's Canadian Light Infantry (11th Re,servo Bat- talion). As many will remember, Private Burdett was educated at the Grammar School, but later left for Canada. Though he has not paid a visit to Lyrnm for five years, he tells us he,finds it very little changed.

Forthcoming Conforence.—On Wednesday, Oetober 6th, Lymm will be honoured by the Cheshire British Women's Temperance Associa- tion, which throughout that day will hold its annual conference in the Primitive Methodist schools. Last year the conference was held at Chester, when the invitation of the Lymm branch (which is about seventy strong) was accepted. It is ,calculated that there will be about two hundred visitors.

"Knows He Is Beaten."—Private Arthur Midden, of the 2nd Border Regiment—writing from the trenches in France to his mother at Pepper-street—mentions that they have been having some nice weather just lately, but it gets very cold at night. "But I suppose we can't expect anything else at this time of the year," he continues, and speaks of the war: "I wonder how long this is going to last? It is about time the Kaiser threw his lot in, as he knows he is beaten, and I don't think he can last much longer." In another letter he says: "We have teen haring a rough time lately."

Lady E v ang eI i st's Visit.—Considerable en- thusiasm was manifested on Monday of last week in a visit by Mrs. Redford (of Manchester) a well-known lady evangelist, who has on different occasions visited the Primitive Metho- dist Church. Addressing a large crowd in that schoolroom, Mrs. Redford emphasised the necessity of everybody being in union one with another; and mentioned the example of the human body. If a finger was hurt the other members of the body afforded comfort and helped it to recover. In the same way, she fell thankful that she had a son guarding the East Coast, another in the Dardanelles, and another in France; becduse that fact 'helped her better to comfort others in similar circum- stances. The leader was Mr. Wm. Downward, of the Christian Endeavour Society (which had invited Mrs. Redford to speak). At the con- clusion Mr. Ernest Moss heartily thanked the speaker for her address.

Y 'VI

DR. DE JONG LEAVES TO SERVE THE TROOPS.

During this week Lymm has lost for .a time one of its most popular medical men—Dr. Edward Meyer De Jong, who, under .the auspices of the tritish Medical Association, has gone to work amongst the soldiers for at least six months.

As medical officer for the Parish of Lymm to the Knutsford -Board of Guardians, Dr. Leonard Thorpe has been appointed in his place pro tem.; and Dr. Thorpe also, we understand, will be appointed to a similar position under the Urban District Council—subject.to the approval of the Local Government Board.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE SONS IN ARMY.

Additions To Our List Of Patriotic Families.

Since the publication last week of our list of, five families which had three sons with the Colours, particulars have come to hand of three additions:—

===== 0002601_19150925_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
The Hadden Family.

Of the three sons of Mrs. Hadden, Pepper- street, two—Walter and Ernest—are abroad with the Lymm Territorials. Walter, whose tome is at Park View, Statham, has been twice wounded. The third son is in the regulars. Private Walter Haddon was called up on the war outbreak, having at the time been a "-Ter- rier" for three years. His age is 25. At the same time Lance:Corporal Ernest (aged 22 and single) had been in for 44 years.

The third—Private Arthur—is unmarried and

aged 33, and in peace times is a stoker at Work- ' ington, Cumberland—where ho has been em- ployed for three years. Formerly he served 8 years in the 2nd Cheshires and 5 in the Reserve. About four months ago he re-enlisted in the Border Regiment, and has been at the Front 31 months.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
The Thomasons.

Mrs. Hannah Thomason, of Rush Green, can also boast the distinction. Her sons are Harry, Herbert and Jack; she has also a soldier son- in-law. Private Jack Thomason—who liTes New-road, Lymm Bridge—was recently' wounded whilst, serving with the sth Cheshires, and has this week I,)eer. cptuping furlough at home. The Bietheet Lelih,

Mrs. Lydia Leigh, Cir. West Hyde, has three of her sons soldiers.

Tom (44) served with the South Laridashires through the Boer War, and later was employed at the Latchford Steel Works. In September, 1914, ho left his work (as a labourer on the new bridge over tht clam for Mr. Harry Fairclough), joining the Warwickshires. He •is now train- ing the troops at Great Crosby. John (30) works at the Latchford Steel Works. and lives at Moston-street with his wife and child. In October, 1914, he enlisted in the South Laneashires, Kitchener's Army, and at the Dar- danelles has been fighting for about three months.

George (27)—unmarried. and also an employe

at the Latchford Steel Works—now lies ill in hospital in Egypt. Like the Hadden brothers, all three were born in Lymm and edncated at the village school.

STATHAM BAZAAR.

Result Of School Children's Patriotic

Effort.

In their schoolroom two weeks ago—as we before reported—the scholars of the Statham Day School held in aid of the wounded soldiers' comforts a small bazaar. With contributions from the scholars, teachers and friends, the stalls were well btocked dead quickly relieved :;f their load. _ _

Other attractions were two short and well- attended entertainments given by the children, and a houp-la table. To all tea was provided. The following contributed gifts of flowers, fruit and refreshments: Mesdames Antrobus, Atherton, Blythe, Coxon, Dalton, Hills, Holt, Inglefield, James, Pigott, Proud, Sharpies,' A. Struthers, J. Struthers, Watson and Wirrell ; Misses Dalton, Blinston, Dixon, Knott, Lance- ley and Leach; and Mr. G. Clark. The amount realised totalled £6 2s. 6d. With this comforts in the form of cigarettes and chocolates have been forwarded to thirty Statham men serving in the Army; handker- chiefs and a small donation handed to Brook- field Red Cross Hospital, and the remainder used in the purchase of wool which the scholars will knit into socks and scarves to meet the winter needs of local men at the Front..
