HARVEST FESTIVALS.

Financial Record At Primitive Methodist Church.

Except for one celebration many years ago, this year's harvest festival at the Primitive Methodist Church has proved the financial record in its history. Held on Sunday, the church had for the occasion been prettily decorated by the young people, and large con- gregations obtained—especially at the evening service. _ _

The preacher was the Rev. W. Daw (of Altrincham), second minister of the church— who took for his subject "Giving," and said that when reaping the corn they should never scratch up the last grain, but should leave a little in the corners of the field for the poor and needy. They could always help somebody poorer than themselves. This applied also to Church life; let them use the gifts of God to them, and He would account it at the Great Reaping. Of this morning sermon the text was Leviticus xxiii., 22: "And when ye reap the harvest of your land, thou shalt not make clean riddance of the corners of thy field when thou reapest, neither shalt thou gather any gleaning of thy harvest, thou shall leave them unto the poor and to the stranger. lam the Lord your God."

At the evening service the choir rendered th? anthems : "Let The People Praise Thee" and "Oh, Taste And See." Miss Florence Shaw presided at the organ. In the schoolroom on Monday evening was held a public tea, to which about seventy sat down. Afterwards the company adjourned tc the church, where the choir gave a service of song entitled "Bob's Birthright." Miss Shaw again supplied the organ accompaniment, whilst the soloists were :. Miss Dora Cross (" God's

Judgments" contralto), and Miss, Bessie Cross "The Power Of Love" soprano). In the con- cluding anthem (" The Earth Is The Lord's ") Miss Eva Tonge took the treble solo. For thcir creditable performance the choir were gener- ally praised. At the conclusion the flowers, !relit, &c., were sold for the benefit of the church f

THE EXAMiNtalt• SATTAWAX, SEPTEMBER 25, 1915.

LYMM TERRITORIALS.

STILL GUALIZING VILLAGE-- " DOING US ALL GOOD." During the last three or four weeks the Lymm Territorials have made no move at the Iront, and continue to .guar the village near the wood where their -last casualties took place in a skirmish with the enemy. Corporal JaCk. 11. MOSthil. of Newfields, in a ... letter home dated September 16th, says: '1 am keeping very well and going on nicely. We are having very nice weather and sits simply lovely here; I think it is doing us all good. We are in -a nice little village, and you., would not believe the fruit that is here. The trees are simply %crOwded: Of course we dare not toiich them. -Onr work is guarding the village and patrolling a very big wood which leads us to a -German village. I would rather have this kind of work than being in the trenches; its much livelier. . ... • I don't get much time for writing just at present; I am hoping to see you all be- fore long." Private Henry Brookes, in a letter to his mother at 1, West Hyde, says: "I was glad to ; hear that Jim [Taylor] had come home for , .good. You must excuse my delay in not writing 1 sooner; I expected that Jim Gilberts was com- ing 'home, but leave was ,cancelled at the last mOment. . . . When I Ot the ribbon to the I medal [4th Order of the Cross of St. George] I will get some of the same colour, and then you ; can have that which is given to me as a kePp- sake:"

===== 0002601_19150925_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
A Good Sign.

Private Percy Clare (Mining Section), writes to Miss B. Cross: "We have all had to send a paper in to-day telling how long we have been in .this country, and asking for a leave, as our company is starting very soon now. I think those who have been out the longest will get the first chance—l hope so, as Joe [Clare] and myself will stand a good chance. "I think we shall get it sooner through being with this lot, as they are going to send about 26 at once."

Yesterday word from Private Clare ran: "It is Sunday morning once again and we are off work to-day. We came down from the trenches at nine o'clock this morning, and we go up again to-morrow Morning at nine. . . We had not any post yesterday and to-day, but we might get it to-night."

Corporal Edwin Blease, Lance-Corporal Sid Linley, and Privates Richard Shaw and Arthur Hadden have this week paid a visit home be- fore returning to Chester en route for the Front. The first three were, of course, wounded some months ago; whilst Private Hadden was accidentally, hurt. All are now again passed for active service. .

Pte. Walter Hadden (twice wounded), Writes on Sunday morning from the 23rd General Hospital, Etaples, France: "I am mending very nicely, and don't think it will be long before I shall be all right again. When I get discharged from the hospital I shall be sent to Rouen to the Convalescent Camp, and from there back to the firing line to the regiment. I would have liked to be sent home, but I suppose I shall have to go back and do a bit more. . I have just been to church—which they bold in one of the wards—and it was very nice." Private Jim Taylor arrived home last week from the Lincoln Hospital, his b.ft arm still being useless for military purposes. He has not, however, been discharged from tbe Army, but has to consider himself on furleagh "from Chester" till further notice.

PRIVATE JACK STATHANT.

Slightly Grazed On _ Head At The

Dardanelles..

Private Joseph Daniels, who has just arrived from the Dardanelles, says that his comrade, Prlvate Jack Statham, of the 6th South Lancashire,s, was slightly grazed by shrapnel on the head, and he left him on a hospital ship. Private Statham has not himself written home 'on the matter to his wife, who lives in Moston-street.

"Probably he will by now" (Private Daniels says), "be back in the firing. line."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOSEPH DANIELS.

Arrives At Lord Derby War Hospital,

Winwieit.

Early on Monday morning Private Joseph Daniels, of the 6th South Lancashires, arrived at the Lord Derby War Hospital, Winwick. As we previously reported, he was wounded in the vicinity of the Dardanelles—through the right hand by gunshot. Previously he had been in hospital at Alexandria, and he landed in England only on Sunday afternoon. His wife was informed of his arrival by post- card from the hospital authorities to his home at 6, The Rookery, and on Tuesday afternoon paid him a visit. She found him in good health and spirits, well provided for, and very glad to be so near home. He does not expect to leave the hospital for some some, as his hand is seriously hurt. Already he has had two bones .eitracted, and there is still some shrapnel dust left in.

By occupation Private Daniels is a tanner.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
BACK AGAIN.

Private James Booth Writes From The

Front.

As we recently reported, Private James Booth, of the Ist Welsh Regiment, after a four -lays' visit home to Cross-brow, Statham— returned a fortn,ight ago to the firing line in Prarice.

In a letter to Councillor Lockyer, of the Star Hotel; he now writes: "Well, I must tell you I arrived here quite safe and am once more 'within sound of the roar of the guns. It is awful, day and night; but never mind—we are still winning! We have got them groggy.' I only wish they would come -out and just see what we are made of; they would soon want to get back again in their dug-outs, you can bet! "We are expecting to go somewhere in a day or two. We do not know where .till we get there; they won't tell us anything. But if I get a chance you can rest assured I will try and see George .Appleton's grave. "I have not come across any of the Lymm lads yet, but am expecting to see them in my travels. I saw the two Percivals [of Booth's Hill] the day after I got back, and they are keeping up their hearts, as it is no use getting downhearted. I must tell you I also saw Sergeant Hewitt [the writer's brother-in-law] and he is all right."

LVIVItVi VOLUNTEERS.

Successes At Appleton Marksmanship

Conipetitions.

This afternoon the local platoon of the Cheshire Volunteers is to parade at Ashton-on- Mersey. Last Saturday some distinction was earned for them at the Appleton Shooting Competitions. In these there were 250 entries; and of the 32 prizes four were carried off by local Volunteers —Q.-M.-Sergt. Hughes (2), Mr. Parker (.1), and Mr. Hawthorn (1). There were six Lymm repre- sentatives present. All the marksmen have now received badges depicting a cartridge running through the.letter "M "—to be worn on the left arm above the elbow.

STOCKTON HEATH.

Welcoming a New Minister.—On Monday evening the new minister at the Bold-street Church, Warrington, the Rev. Percy meson, made the sacquaintance of the Stockton Heath Wesleyans at a well-attended recognition meet- ing held in their Church, which still wore the decorations put up for the previous day's harvest feitival. The new minister was accompanied by Mrs. meson. A short address of welcome was given by the Rev. W. Thompson Derry (the superintendent minister of tile Warrington Circuit), the new minister suitably expressing his thanks and his pleasure at meeting the friends of Stockton Heath. During the evening solos were sung by the Misses Houghton and Taylor, whilst the Misses Webb contributed a duet. The choir rendered an appropriate anthem. Ladies of the congregation dispensed light refreshments and fruit, and altogether a thoroughly enjoyable evening was spent.

Wesleyan Harvest Festival.—Successful har- vest festival services were held at the local Wesleyan Church on Sunday, good congrega- tions being the order of the day. Special hymns and anthems were sung morning, afternoon and evening, the choir being under the conductor- ship of Mr. F. E. Penketh, the choirmaster. The preacher at the morning service was Mr. F. Wright. In the afternoon the Rev. A. J. Morton gave an appropriate address at a special young people's service, at which recita- tions and dialogues were given by the Sunday school children. The Rev. A. J. Morton also occupied the pulpit in the evening. Mr. G. A. Whitfield officiate(' at the organ throughout the day. The church was tastefully decorated with corn, fruit and flowers, some of the fruit and flowers being subsequently sent to the Raddon Court Hospital for the benefit of the wounded soldiers quartered there. The remainder of the fruit was sent to a soldier member of the church who is lying sick in a military hospital in Kent.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATE.

Coming Visit of Dr. Ballard.—The Padgate Wesleyans are to be congratulated on securing the distinguished services of. the Rev. .Frank Ballard. He preaches on Sunday, October 3rd and lectures on Monday, October 4th. Dr. Ballard is a brilliant scholar and philosophic writer. Probably no man living has done more to popularise the arguments in defence of the Christian Faith. He is a man of broad views and living questions are fearlessly discussed. His open conferences at the close of his lectures, whilst revealing great erudition and masterly argumentative power are always marked by great courtesy to his questioners. ,

Wounded Soldiers Entertained.—About forty wounded soldiers from the Lord Derby Hos- pital, Winwick, were entertained on Thursday afternoon by the members of the Padgate Tennis and Bowling ‘Club. The men were brought from the hospital in a char-a-banc, and Mr. R. Tunstall kindly lent his motor, and Mr. Duck- worth his trap for the occasion. An appetising meal had been prepared for the 'visitors by the lady members of the club, and the men thoroughly enjoyed the good things provided. Mr. Duckworth, in a short speech, gave a hearty welcome to the visitors who spoke in glowing terms of the excellent work they had done in the trenches. The members, he said, were try- ing to do a little towards making their lives happier during the time of convalescence. A vote of thanks to the ladies was moved by Mr. Duckworth and seconded by the Rev. A. J. Morton. Bowls, tennis, croquet, &c., were played by the soldiers, who showed their appreciation for the kindness shown them dur- ing the afternoon. Harvest Festival.—The harvest festival ser- vices in connection with the Wesleyan Church were held on Sunday last. The church had been very tastefully decorated for the occasion, and the gifts of fruit, flowers, &c., were more numerous than usual owing to the fact that same were to be distributed amongst the wounded soldiers in the Winwick Hospital. The preacher in the morning was the, Rev. Percy meson, who took as his text "Except a corn of wheat fall into the ground and die, it abideth alone; but if it die, it bringeth forth much fruit." The anthem was "The Earth is the Lord's" ( (Simper), and the solo was ably sung by Mr. W. Seed. A very interesting address was given to the children in the afternoon by the Rev. W. T. Derry, who also 'conducted the evening service, the text being "I am, the bread of life." The choir sang the anthem "Fear Not, 0 Lord" (Simper), Mr. W. J. Bate being the 'soloist. A quartet, "They That Sow in Tears" was also sweetly rendered by Mrs. W. J. Bate, Miss Gilbert and Messrs. C. Sinclair and W. Seed. Mr. W. H. Ward presided at the organ. The collections, which were very satisfactory, were in aid of the Circuit and Trust funds. The Time To Be Alive.—The temperance meet- ing of the Wesley Guild was held on Thursday, when an address was given by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, vicar of Burtonwood. He said he was glad to have another opportunity of visiting Padgate. He had heard it said that Padgate was a very progressive district, and he firmly believed that the Wesley Guild was the fore-

most progressive institution in the district. Speaking on the temperance question he re- marked that in spite of the war, drinking still goes on. There was still great need for the ex-

tension of controlled areas. The temperance communities should work together, as now is the time, for us all to be alive, as we have machinery calculated to strike such a blow at the drink traffic that it will never recover. He also spoke of the good work that is being done fbr the temperance question and our soldiers by the,Y.M.C.A. and the soldiers' rest in connection with the Bold-street Wesleyan Church. He advocated thrift to help on the State and our brave " Tommies," and advised all to 'practise self-sacrifice and self-denial in regard to thrift. Referring to the Budget, he expressed surprise that the temperance ques- tion had not been mentioned by the Chancellor. We are all spiritual beings working together for one object. Our possibilities are unlimited and our work will go forward in spite a all opposition and eventually meet with the success It do richly deserves.

A capital musical programme had been ,arranged, a trio "Lift Thine Eyes" (" Elijah ") being sung by Misses L. Mason, J. Ward and Mrs. L. Bennett. A solo entitled "The Carni- val" was' sung by Mr. H. Walker, and Mr. F. Moon contributed "The Village Blacksmith." Duets entitled "The Moon Hath Raised Her Lamp Above" and "Now Are We Ambassa- dors" (" St. Paul "), were also capably rendered by Messrs. 11. Walker and F. Moon. The Rev. A. J. Morton presided and Mrs. W. J. Bate officiated at the piano. Votes of thanks to the speaker and artistes were proposed by Messrs. W. E. Plinston and J. Seed.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
GRADING APPLES.

The " Telegraph" devotes a considerable space to apples and the apathy of English people about them—that is, the early ones. With much gassy talk, the old statement is re- peated that the Cox's Orange Pippin is " indis- putably " the finest apple in the world.. This is not true. The apple that answers to that description is the Newtown Pippin, so far as known.

There ,is • a large consUming populace for such well-known kinds as Beauty of Bath, Lady Sudeley, Devonshire Quarrenden, Irish Peach, and Gladstone. Epicures who enjoy none but perfect apples will not rhapsodise over any of the aforenamed, which, however, are the best obtainable at this time of year. Within the last week or so there has been a striking example of the value of good packing and grading in regard to apples. One grower, Mr. Arthur Miskin, of Chart-Sutton, near Maid- stone, has always professed a firm belief in sending apples to market attractively packed in boxes. In other words, he has thought fit to adopt the methods which prevail in America and in some of the Colonies. And with what result? Quite recently his best samples of Beauty of Bath realised as much as 30s. a box the equivalent 'of a bushel in capacity. This price is fully three times the sum that exactly the same fruit would have fetched in bushels or half-bushels. Evidently the trouble taken in grading and packing is well recompensed, as Mr. Miskin prepares five separate grades in Beaiity of Bath alone. For the fifth selection (the smaller fruit) as much as 15s. a box has been paid. At the time of writing, however, the value is not more than los., but the selling agent is certain that the identical apples less carefully prepared would realise no more than 3s. for the same weight of fruit".

RUNCOI.RN RURAL COUNCIL.

Half-Yearly Budget Requires A Sixperin3,l Rate.

Mr. T. Antwis occupied the chair at the monthly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, which was held at Dutton on Tuesday, when there was but little business of public interest awaiting transaction, the meeting last- ing but a few minutes.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
ESTIMATED EXPENSES OVER £6,500.

The Acting Clerk (Mr. W. a Hough) pre- smited the estimates for the ensuing half-year, which showed that general expenses, . including

_ highway charges, were expected to total £6,648-. Contingencies were put at £3,512. Deducting the anticipated balance on September 30th, there was left a net expenditure to be provided for of £4,979. A rate of 6d. in the £ produced £4,991, and it was proposed to levy a rate to meet that amount. The Acting Clerk added that the rate proposed was the same as in the last half-year, and the half-year before that.

The Chairman: Thatjwill be gratifying to you all, I suppose. (Hear, hear.) The estimates were accepted.

"HOIST WITH HIS OWN PETARD,"

The parishes of Kingsley, Frodsham and Latchford Without were reported to be in arrears in the matter of. satisfying the call for rates made upon them by the overseers. The Chairman: 1 hope Latchford Without will pay up A Member: What price Kingsley?

A smile ran round the table as the members recalled the fact that the Chairman comes from r.nd represents Kingsley.

M.O.H. ON THE SICK LIST.

The Medical Officer of Health (Dr, .1. Adams) wrote from London regretting that a sudden return of ulceration trouble compelled him to cease work for a few weeks and probably to undergo 'another operation. He had been unable to obtain a locum tenens, but Dr. Joseph or his colleague, or Dr. Burton would act for him when required during his absence. The doctor added that he hoped that his enforced absence would only be about the dura- tion of his usual holiday. The arrangement,suggested by, the doctor dur- ing his temporary absence was approved.

RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

Slight Increase In Half. Yearly

Rate.

Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presided at the usual fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians, which was held at the Dutton Work- house on Tuesday morning.

RATEPAYERS TO FIND NEARLY £20,000. The most important business before the meeting was the presentation of the estimates of expenditure for the ensuing half-year, which were presented by the Clerk. The probable expendi- ture was given at £19,853, made up of common fund expenses £6,289 (less probable receipts estimated at £3,020) ; county rate £15,165; con- tingencies fund £1,853, special education pur- poses £962. The assessable value of the Union is estimated at £261,552; and-a rate of Is. sd. in the 2 will produce £18,512. The precept for Runcorn totalled - £4,850. The Acting Clerk (Mr. W. E.. Hough) said that the rate would show a slight increase— little short of a halfpenny, in fact, as com- pared with the last half-year's figure. That was due to an increase in the county expendi- ture.

The Chairman: Our expenditure is quite normal.

Tho estimates were passed.,

PATRIOTIC WORKHOUSE MASTER.

After discussion in camera the Board decided to grant the Master (Mr. E. F. Parsons) per- mission to join H.M. Forces provided that the House Committee are satisfied that suitable arrangements can be made for the proper running of the establishment during his absence.

Statistics submitted by the Master showed that during the first week of the past fortnight the inmates of the Workhouse numbered 118, as against 136 in the corresponding period of last year. ===== 0002601_19150925_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
ECONOMY IN WAR-TIME.

Schools For Women Of Moderate Means.

There has been in existence for some time a National Thrift Campaign and, whilst one admires the energy, the good maxims and the endeavours to obtain support, one cannot help

feeling that there has been behind the move-- ment much more preaching than practice. It is therefore with greater pleasure we call atten- tion to the existence in our midst of schools for women, where the wives of our working men can go to receive instruction in the arts of cookery, dressmaking and millinery, from highly qualified teachers who have spent their lives in learning to make money go as far as possible. These classes are not for persons who desire to learn how to cook a six course dinner, or who require dresses and hats for various occasions. They are essentially for those who find it hard to make both ends meet, and, from inside knowledge, we are sure that as a result of attending the classes their task of making ends meet will not only be easier but there will be an overlap, which can be kept for future requirements. Free Lectures on Domestic Economy.

The teachers will be in attendance at the Beamont, Evelyn-street and Bolton Schools on Tuesday next, for the purpose of enrollinr, students for the coming Session. Arrangement; have been made for free lectures in economical cookery to be given at these schools, and women intending to he present must give in their names to the teachers next Tuesday, so that arrange- ments can be made for all.

We sincerely hope that this practical attempt' to teach economy may prove a huge sucscess, awl that we shall hear of such demand that the number of sr3hoots will have to he increased.

PEARSON An KNOWLES.

Trade" Precarious And Surrounded With Difficulty."

The following report of the directors of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., to the forty-second annual meeting of share- holders, to be held at the Company's Office in Warrington next 'W%dnesday, states The profits for the year ending 30th June, 1915, amount to 1:122,359, ss. 2d., and the balance brought forward from last year is ..t21,842 is. 5d., together £144,201 6s. 7d. An interim dividend at the rate of 6 per cent. per annum on the first and second preference shares was paid in March last for the half-year ending December 31st, 1914, amounting to £10,507 Bs. sd. The directors now recommend that a dividend be paid for the past half-year at the rat* of 6 per cent. per annum on the first and second preference shares, and a dividend on the ordinary shares of 7i per cent. for the year, making with the interim dividend a total dis- tribution of £62,386 9s. 2d. The directors further recommend that .247,500 be added to the reserve fund, raising. it to -R450,000; that the sum of .R 8,254 is. 5d., which represents the expenditure on capital account for the year, be written off, leaving a balance of £26,060 16s. to be carried forward.

The special reserve of £lO,OOO set aside from last year's profits for the purpose of meeting con- tingencies, has been appropriated, as proposed at the last meeting, to assisting the wives and families of those of the company's workmen who have joined the Army, and to certain provisions to which the directors- then referred. The business of the company has been con- ducted with difficulty, which has been much aggravated by shortage of labour, both skilled and Inskilled, in collieries, iron works and wire mills.

A substantial contribution to the year's pro- fits has been received from the company's shares in the Partington Steel and Iron Co., Ltd., the development of whose business is now making satisfactory progress. The present position of the iron and coal tradte, in common with the general business of the country, is precarious, and surrounded with difficulty, and the future it is impossible to foresee.

In compliance with the Articles of Association, Major H. Harmood Banner retires from the Board, and is eligible for re-election. AN EXCEPTIONALLY GOOD YEAR.

The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company has had an exceptionally good year, says the "Manchester Guardian," although the directors say its ordinary business has been con- ducted with difficulty, which was much aggra- vated by shortage of labour alike at the collieries, ironworks and wire mills. In 1909-10 the net profit was £66,617 (dividend 31.- per cent.), in 1910-11 £70,978 (dividend 4 per cent.), and in 1911-12 £78,141 (dividend 5 per cent). ===== 0002601_19150925_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
THE lIARV.EST IN FRANCE.

Hurry Causes Loss To An Abundant Crop.

This year in France there can be no waiting on the weather. Wet or fine, there were certain days appointed for the harvest. There is scarcely a man of ripe labouring age left in France upon the land, and few are the women who can drive the great reaping machines across the heavy soil. One day, indeed, at Ormessan, early in July, I saw three men reaping in a field and stopped to talk with them. The farmer was fifty, and the two lads between seventeen and eighteen; they were cuting the held full early in order to get it done before the expected sum- mons should call them to their regiment. But, as a rule, the harvesters this year were not the resident labourers of the farm. The Army has done well and wisely in giving abundant harvest leave, and in this sole department of Seine-et- Oise, thanks to the combined efforts of the Pre- fect and the officers, 4,800 soldiers were occupied during July and early August in cutting and carrying the harvest of the region. Only, of course, these "permissions de moisson " are rightly limited in time and place—five days, eight days, seldom more, in such-and-such a commune, and then the harvester rejoins his regiment. So many a field was cut in the wet, and many a soaking storm fell down on the great fields studded and starred with cocks of corn, that had to wait their turn to be carried, for there is at least as great a penury of horses as of men. The golden straw and the splendid full ears began to blacken, the warm insides 9f the sheaves showed germinating grains, and our hearts sank within us. Much harm, no doubt, was done, for most of the corn was carried (the plough already is furrowing many a field) be- fore the sun came out again in mid-August. And now the thrashing machines began to drone in rivalry with the aeroplanes overhead. It is only when these great " batteuses " have finished their work that we shall know exactly how we stand. - But we begin to see already that, though a portion of what would have been so fine a crop is spoiled and useless, the re- mainder is fairly abundant and of excellent quality.—" Country Life."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
HOURS OF MILKING.

Milking the cows on a cold, dark morning is I not a very agreeable operation. It is hurried, the cows are not always stripped, and thelmen are not always clean and good-tempered. 1 was recently on a farm in the West, where two men are in charge of some forty head, drawing the milk by machine, and speaking most highly of its work, which they prefer to using their hands. On this farm the engine which runs the milking machine also works a dynamo, and the whole 'building is lit by the electric light, the lamps being suspended above the central passage, with the cows right and left. I do not believe that any farmer would find any further difficulty arise if he obtained this installation, which is so agreeable to the milkers and the cows. The fear of prosecution for selling milk below the standard may be practically abolished by milking twelve hours apart. Where the cows are milked, for example, at '6 a.m. and 4 p.m., the evening's milk is enriched at the ex- pense of that of the morning; thus the morn- ing milk is often so poor that the 'farmer runs a serious risk of prosecution. If, however, the milking is twelve hours apart, the morning milk is brought up to the quality of that of the evening, or very near it. Again, the number of cows which produce milk below the standard in the morning is in the one ease frequently numerous, while in the other it means nothing at all.--Professor LOU& in " Farm and Horne."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC APPOINTMENTS.

Sir,—l noticed in your report of the proceedings of the Warrington Rural District Council at its last meeting that the matter of the appointment of a medical officer for the district came up for discussion. No notice, I believe, had been given the members of the Council that on appointment would be made, and the matter was rushed through in a most unusually hasty manner.

It would seem to me, having regard to an important appointment of this character that at least the vacancy should have been adver- tised and medical men in the district given. tho opportunity of applying. I will not dwell upon the fact that the pro- visional appointment is given to a gentleman. who lives out of the district, and who already holds several public appointments. It would seem to me that men who are paid for publis work of this character should at least live amongst the people they are expected to serve, and who find the rates to pay. The residence of the gentleman who is pro- visionally appointed is in Culcheth, outside ther rural district altogether, and at least six ranee from Penketh, Sankey and Burtonwood, and at least two miles from the nearest point of Pad- gate.

WIN WICK.

ORFORD TANNERY FATALITY.

Widow Awarded ,Zl9O Compensation.

Before his Honour Judge J. Scott Fox, KC., at the Warrington County Court on Thursday morning, Mrs. Kate Lilian Dolan, of Sandy lane Cottage, Orford, sought compensation, under the Workmen's Compensation Act, for the loss of her husband, Michael Dolan, who recently died as the result of an accident which befel him on his employers' premises.

A CASE FOR COMPROMISE. Mr. Greaves Lord, who was instructed by Mr. Pr, S. Steel, appeared for the respondents, and said that the deceased was employed by Messrs. Wm. Mortimer and Co., tanners, of Orford, as a hide roller. The accident which caused his death occurred whilst he was said to be doing something which was not his work at all; v 4., putting a belt on a machine which was used not for the rolling, but for the polish- ing of hides. There was a very serious question as to whether the accident arose out of, or in the course of, his employment. The total amount that might have been recoverable was £245, but in view of the evidence which he would be able to call, his learned friend had agreed to a compromise in the shape of an award for £l9O and certain costs, also agreed upon. The amount named had been paid into Court. The deceased left a widow and one child of his own, and there were also two step- children.

THE JUDGE'S AWARD.

Dr. T. Eastham, instructed by Messrs, B. Davies and Co. for the widow, assented to an award on the lines mentioned by Mr. Greaves Lord, and his Honour accordingly made an order for the payment of £l9O and the agreed costs.

A LOST FINGER,

County Court Judge Reduces

Riveter's Compensation.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday application was made to his Honour Judge J. Scott Fox., K.C., on behalf of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., Warrington, ta terminate or diminish the weekly payment which has been made, under the Workmen's Compensation Act, to Thomas Hazlehurst, of 25, Cornwall-street, a former employe, who met with an accident whilst in the company's ser- vice. _ . _ .

===== 0002601_19150925_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
Paid Full Sum Under Act.

Mr. E. Acton said that the applicants, for whom he appeared, were engineers, carry- ing on business at the Dallam Forge, &c., and the respondent had been a riveter in their em- ploy. The application was for the termination or the diminution of the weekly amount of com- pensation at present being paid, that amount being the full amount under the Act, viz.; ..t1 per week. It appeared that on August 13th, 1913, the respondent, who was working as a riveter, sustained injuries by being struck on the left index finger by a hammer, the result being that the finger had had to be amputated— clown to the last joint. Subject to that injury he would call evidence that the man was in perfect health, suffered no pain, and was per- fectly well able to work. Whilst working as a riveter his average earnings were £3 ss. 4d. a week. After the accident the respondent re- mained off work until February 2nd, 1914, when he went back into the applicants' employ, and was given such work as he could do—light work.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
£2 a Week and Light Work.

For that work the firm paid him 21s. a week, plus full compensation of .21 per week, making a total of .22 is. That went on for a year, and in February, 1915, the applicants, thinking that the man might make an attempt to do his own Work, offered him his old job as a riveter back again, or, in the alternative, work as a holder- up, at which he would earn only about 4s. per week less than at his own occupation. The man, however, refused, saying that he did not think he was able to do the work; and on March 2nd of the present year he left the applicants' em- ployment and went to work for Messrs. Wands Bros., Ltd. The reason he left was that at the time the applicants gave their workers a war bonus of 35., but they did not give it to the respondent, because he was receiving compensa- tion. For a short time the man worked for Messrs. Rylands as a labourer at an average wage of Ms. 4d., and then he was put on to drilling, for which he was paid 275. a week, with a 3s. war bonus. With 'overtime he had aver- aged 31s. 101 d. a week, which was about 335. sd. below his previous weekly earnings. Splitting the Difference.

Under circumstances such as those, the general rule was that the difference between the man's earnings, and what he had earned on the average prior to the accident, should be divided between the employer and the workman, and if that rule was followed, it would mean that the man should be paid 16s. Bd. per week instead of oeil that was assuming that his -Honour held that the man was still incapacitated. The sub- mission he had to make that day was that if the man would make up his mind to put forth a genuine and conscientious effort he could do his old work perfectly well. But he seemed to be possessed with the idea that he could not do the work and never would be able to do it. Lost the Power of Guidance.

Medical and other testimony having been called on both sides, the respondent himself went into the box and said he could not now do the work of a riveter, because his accident had deprived him of the power of guiding his ham- mer; indeed, he would be a source of danger to the man he worked with, his grip having been greatly reduced. He could swing a hammer, but could not depend on hitting the object he Was aiming at. Had he been able to follow his imvn trade he could be making about -25 or £6 week, instead of about 306., plus 20s, compensa- tion.

Dr. Eastham, for the respondent, urged that it was unlikely that unless his client had been really rendered incapable of doing his old work„ That he would be content to accept such a very ilauch smaller wage on more .menial work; to lost, in fact, somewhere between and ..t3 every week. - _ -

His Honour, in giving judgment, said that he -thought the respondent was still suffering dis- e!bility as the result of his. permanent injury, and he did not think he was, at once, capable of resuming his work as a riveter; but he did think that he might try. He reduced the amount of the award to 17s. per week.

IN THE COURTS.

BROKE IN AT NIGHT AND TOOK

BABY.

Unusual Episodes Narrated In Assault

Case.

"As soon as I see him in his drink I have to fly; I am terrified." The words were spoken at Tuesday's Police Court during a case heard by Messrs. J. Rymer Young (in the chair) and C. Jackson Holmes. Elizabeth Syres summoned her husband, John Henry Syres, a well-spoken bar wheeler, of 10, Manley-street, for assault on September 11th; and defehdant, in pleading guilty, said he was "very sorry about it." Mr. A. Browne, on behalf of complainant, said if defendant would plead guilty to aggra- vated assault, they would not press for im- prisonment, but would ask for a separation, as he was not a safe man to live with—they could see her eye which had been hit ten days before. What Happened At Theatre.

BlizabethliSyres said she married defendant on April 3rd, 1908, at St. Barnabas' Church. On Saturday, September 11th, they went to- gether to the Royal Court Theatre and went in the circle. It was crowded and she had just room to sit down on a. step. He stood up in front of two young ladies and they asked him to move. She pulled the corner of his coat to get him to move, and then he walked out with- out a word. As she thought .he ,would go drink- ing she followed him and found him in the "Old House At Home." She tried to persuade him to go back to the theatre, but he wOuld not. They went home through the Market, where they bought a rabbit. He insisted on calling at the Vine Tavern for a meat pie and beer, and she went with him to do what she could.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Young Ladies Sneering.

Later he went into the Coopers' Arms and insisted on her coming with him. Whilst they were there two young ladies began sneering at her and he took their part. They (husband and wife) went home past the Infirmary, and as they Were passing she said: "You have no respect for me or you wouldn't have spoken to those two." "Oh,". he said, "if your - parents fell out with them I won't." He thumped her and she ran away from him, but he caught her and thumped her all the way to Froghall Bridge; she was running out of his way all the time. She had to give up at the bridge as she was exhausted, and he held her against the wall and thumped her again. When she got into his aunt's (where they lived) he had her against the cupboard in the kitchen and she (the aunt) told him to leave her alone. She sat at the corner of the table and told him he ought to be at the Front, and he "let out" and hit her in the eye; she could not open it till Tuesday morning, and was Attended by Dr. Liston. He went to his sister's in Elizabeth- street:

===== 0002601_19150925_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
In Nightdress To Stockton Heath.

On Saturday night she. went out with her mother at half-past nine to buy some . meat, leaving her baby, three years old, in bed. When she came back she found it had gone. He had broken in 'through the door and taken it away in its nightdress. They rang through to Stock- ton Heath Police Station and found it; he had taken it all the way to Stockton Heath. Her brother brought it back the next- morning in borrowed clothes. She (complainant) had never slept once this week, and had eaten nothing at all.

Defendant: Do you think it right for me to come every night and have no tea ready? Complainant: Sir, that is a lie. Defendant: Did I come home and find you speecless drunk? Complainant: That is a lie, sir. Mr. Browne: Ile doesn't deny the evidence, sir!

Later defendant said he was heartily sorry for what he had done..

The Chairman then explained that defendant should be charged with aggravated assault, and the man said 'he understood. Practically • A Beast.

Mr. Browne: This man iA practically a beast and is continually striking his wife. For two and a half years past she has on three occasions had to sleep out with:neighbours,, and once his aunt had to threaten to knock him on the head with a elate if he did not stop. He was convicted at the Stockton Heath Police Court on a charge of being drunk and disorderly and fined five shillings and costs; he was then so disgusted with himself that he threw up a good job and asked his wife to go with him to Glasgow—as an ironworker can get employment anywhere. She consented when he promised to be good and behave better. For a few weeks he was good, and then behaved so badly to her that her brother went over, found he had been assault- ing her, and took her away with him to Stock- bridge. Defendant wrote repeatedly asking her to come back, and at last, after five months, got Father Rockliffe to write a very beautiful letter saying if she would come back her husband would try to be a better man. She came back to him, and ever since he had thumped her— usually about.the breast and arms. Her arms were bruised now. "Cut His Hands Off."

Last week defendant came to Mr. Laws (the Police Court Missioner) and said he was very sorry for what he had done and could cut his hands off. He asked Mr. Laws to, keep him out of prison. Yesterday, however, he was seen helplessly drunk. His aunt would tell the Bench that he was not a fit and proper person for the girl to live with. The wife corroborated and said she was really terrified to live with him. His standing wage as a bar wheeler was 30s. a week from Mr. Walsh, contractor, of Dallara Forge, and 3s. a week bonus. In addition he earned over 9s. a week, making file-boxes. She asked for 12s. 6d. a week to keep the child and herself. Wants To Keep Respectable.

Defendant: I can't pay her 12s. 6d. a week; It is impossible. I have had to give the extra money up. There isn't a happier couple if she would only keep away from her parents; she is altogether there. It costs me 14s. for my lodgings, and I want to keep respectable. The Chairman: I suppose it doesn't matter how your wife and child look! You will be fined 40s. for aggravated assault, and the separation is granted. Your wife will have the custody of the child, and you must pay her 12s. 6d. a week.

" Do Something Dangerous."

Bottomley said that on the 20th the man called at his office and said he wanted the Guardians to take care of him or otherwise he would bst doing something dangerous. He sent him into the office of Mr. Shearer, whom the man told he wanted an order for the Workhouse on account of drink. "I am not fit to take care of myself." he added. At their meeting the pre- vious day (continued Mr. Bottomley) the Guardians considered the man's case and found that upon three recent occasions he had been upon the Union for various periods suffeakinff from delirium tremens brought on by drink. He worked for the Richmond Company, and dur- ing the last four weeks had earned £1 us. 10d. per week; there was now work there for him to go to if he would. Under those circumstances the Guardians thought the case should be brought before the magistrates because they did not consider it right that an able-bodied man like defendant should throw himself on the Guardians owing to drink. He understood that whilst sober the man was a very good worker, father and husband; but when in drink he was a very, very dangerous man indeed. 1-le (Mr. Bottomley) asked them to consider the case from the point that the man had brought him- self to them chargeable. The Chairman's Fear.

Invalid Wife Applies For Increased

===== 0002601_19150925_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSERVATIVES' TRIBUTES.

Mr. J. Fulton-Smith, speaking at the Con- servative Club last Saturday, at the close of a bowling match, said the club had sustained a severe loss by the death of Mr. John Wells (the chairman of the Bowling Green Com- mittee). To all appearance, when they last saw him, Mr. Wells was likely to live for a long time; but he had passed.away. They would never forget his personality—the earnestness he always introduced into everything he undertook and his evident wish to make all he met on the green enjoy and understand the games. He would be remembered and resliected, not only as a member of the club, but as a bowler; and all present vieould join with him in expressing their sympathy with the relatives in their sad and sudden bereavement

Mr. T. Horrabin spoke of Mr. Wells as one who was ever ready to render any financial assistance which was needed in connection with the green. They had all heard of the sudden death with surprise and regret. He would be very much missed.

_ Mr. Herbert Whittle (secretary of the com- mittee) said it would never be fully known what Mr. Wells had done to promote the suc- cess of *he various competitions held on the green. In times of need he had always come to the rescue. and his death would be a great loss to them all.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
LATE MR. E. S. A, ROBSON. IWITH THE WOODS

Education Committee's "tributel WORKS BOWLING ASSOCIATIO:\ To Capable " Head.,"

The Warrington Education Committee. :It their meeting on Monday night, paid their tribute to the memory of the late Mr. E. S. A. Robson, the principal of the Secondary School. who passed away a few weeks ago after a long and distressing illness. The minutes of the School Management Sub- committee contained the following item: "The death of Mr. E. S. A. Robson, principal of the Secondary School and Technical Institute, having been reported at a meeting of the com- mittee on August 20th, it was resolved that the Director be instructed to convey to Mrs. Robson and the relatives an expiression of the com- mittee's sense of the great loss that has been sustained by the death of Mr. E. S. A. Robson and of the committee's sincere sympathy and condolence with them in their sad bereave- ment.

Mr. J. Upson formally moved the adoption of the minutes of the committee.

ALDERMAN BENNETT'S TRIBUTE.

Alderman Bennett said he could -not allow the occasion to pass without making special refer- ence to the services to local education rendered by the late Mr. Robson. Under his able, skilled, enthusiastic and highly capable manage- ment the Secondary School had won laurels of which it might well be proud. The cigcum- stances of Mr. Robson's death seemed to him (the Alderman) to be particularly pathetic, the vitality with which he stuck to his work until almost the last possible moment being charac- teristic of the man. He had the highest regard for the late Mr. Robson and for the work he had accomplished in the town.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE PRINCIPAL'S SERVICES TO

EDUCATI ON.

The Chairman (Councillor 11. Roberts) said he would like to add his own personal tribute to the ability and to the conscientious assiduity with which the late Mr. Robson carried on his work. As long as he was physi- cally capable he rendered unstinted service to the cause of education and in the interests and for the well being of the children entrusted to his charge. The whole circumstances of his pass- ing were surrounded by a pathos which, fortunately, did not occur too often, for was it not pathetic to see, as they all did, the spectacle of a man stricken down in the very plenitude of his powers, and . to know, as they all knew, that for some considerable time Mr. Robson was suffering from a disease which could only ter- minate in one way; to see his physical disabili- ties borne with a patience that was exemplary; to know that he .was waited upon and nursed by one of the bravest women it had ever been his (the speaker's) pleasure to know; and to remember that he had left two fatherless little lads?

===== 0002601_19150925_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
A Great Loss To The Town.

The loss to the town by the death of Mr. Robson was very great. There would be grow- ing up in Warrington quite a generation of young people whose future had been largely moulded by Mr. Robson's personal attention and ability. The words of the resolution only con- veyed very faintly the feeling of personal loss which every member of the committee felt, but they hoped that it would in some measure afford consolation to the aged father of the deceased gentleman and to his widow and mem- bers of his family to feel that in their great and irreparable loss they had the deepest sym- pathy of the Warrington Education Committee. The minutes were passed.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. KINGDON'S TRIBUTE.

"The success of the Secondary School was very dear to him." This sentence was con- tained in a letter received by the Director from Mr. Holman Kingdon, who went on to express his desire to be associated with the resolution of the committee, and to refer to Mr. Robson's singular unity of purpose and great sense of duty.

SHOOTING COMPETITIONS.

Appleton Rifle Club Promote

interesting Event.

The Appleton Rifle Club held their first open prize meeting on Saturday, when the pro- gramme comprised seven competitions and about ninety competitors were attracted, in- cluded in that number being many members of the local Volunteer Corps. Altogether the entries totalled 275—a figure which was re- garded as extremely satisfacto* and should do much to encourage the club to proceed with their project of m'aking the prize meeting an annual event. The competitions were in progress from 12.30 until after 6 p.m., and, perhaps, most interest centred in the disc competition for teams of three—firing at 50 yards' range—which attracted fourteen trios, among the bodies represented being the War- rington Volunteer Training Corps, the Stock- ton Heath and Lynam detachments of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, the Appleton Club, and the following local rifle clubs:— Local Government Officers'; Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co.'s; Monks, Hall and Co.'s; and the St. Helens Cable Company's.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESENTING THE PRIZES.

At the close of firing, Mrs. Lyon, wife of Lieutenant-Colonel Lyon, of Appleton Hall, presented the prizes won. With her was her son, Lieutenant Frank Lyon, who recently re- ceived a commission in the Grenadier Guards. Before Mrs: Lyon gave the prizes away, Mr. A. Shepherd (chief range officer) said a few words, in the course of which he opined that, in times like the present, there was great need that every man should be able to use a rifle. After Mrs. Lyon had given the prizes, Lieutenant Lyon said a few words of con- gratulation and wished all prosperity to the club.

On the proposition of Mr. F. C. Bennion a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Lyon, the speaker remarking that it was through the generosity of the members of that lady's family that the members of the club had the use of so ideal' a range. Mrs. Lyon briefly expressed her thanks.

AMATEUR ASSOCIATION.

CHAMPIONS v. "THE REST" MEET FOR

CHARITY.

The Senior Conservatives, who are this year's Warrington and District Amateur Bowling Association champions, on Saturday afternoon met a team representative of "The Rest" Of the cltibs affiliated to the Association in the cause of charity, the "gate" money and the re- ceipts from the tickets sold going to swell the funds of the Warrington Infirmary. The match was played on the Sankey-street green, but the "gate" was disappointing. It is hoped, however, that the returns from the sale of tickets will compensate for this lack of support. Two representatives from each club should have played, but owing to closing .mains and other attractions all the men dfil not put in an appearance.

The- first game off was that of S. Massey (Rylands's), who soon disposed of fl. Jolley, but A. Broady squared matters, J. Gleave (Rylands's) 'finding the Sankey-street skipper in his best mood. H. Hughes and D. Knight beat the Walton pair 15-6 each, but J. C. Spilsbury found P. Gleave (Whitecross) a bit too good for him. T. G. Brookes and J. Lodge beat the Pen- keth men 15-12 and 15-8, aid IL Whittle and C. Woods accounted for Walker's couple 15-7 and 15-11. D. Richardson showed his home form and easily beat T. Cooper (Junior Con- servatives). E. Gleave and S. Caldwell gave the Rope and Anchor representatives a rough time, winning 15-4 and 15-3. R. Straw had a tough customer in W. Jones (Whitecross), who won a good game 15-13. H. P. Turner and J. Bil- hinge disposed of the Cemetery men 15-11 and 15-13. J. Woods and T. Clare (Rylands's) both won close games 15-13 and 15-14, but P. Han- kinson and J. Bellian accounted for the King and Queen couple 15-10 and 15-12, the Seniors winning by 76 on the 19 games played.

WOMEN BOWLERS.

Oughtrington Beat Warrington In Fine Contest.

The Warrington ladies who use the Liverpool- road and Latchford greens combined to meet the Oughtrington ladies in matches, away on the 13th inst., and in Warrington on Monday. As the municipal greens provide no accommoda- tion for tea, the local team got permission to use the Conservative Club green in Sankey-street, and not only was some excellent bowling wit- nessed, but the use of the building for tea was highly appreciated. FOOTBALL.

NORTHERN UNION.

Ought Warrington To Have Played

This Season ?

A correspondent writes:— Warringtonians—at least a good majority of those who have been in the habit of spending their Saturday afternoons watching with keen interest the doings of their favourites in the primrose and blue jerseys—will scan with envious eyes the reports of the matches a the various Northern Union clubs who are playing "football as usual."

: Many are the grumbles which one hears with, :regard to the decision come to by the Warring- ton committee in playing junior football only, but, as already stated in these columns, the Warrington committee came to that decision afber very careful consideration—and by that deciSion they will undoubtedly stand. rt is, however, a sore point with many habitues to WiTtierspool, and now that so many of our local works are engaged in the execution of Government contracts, one wonders whether the action of the committee will turn out to be a wise proceecfinx. Surely our workers—even those who are of military age—could be doing many a worse thing than indulging in a game of Rugby football during their spare hours! And what better recreation can a man have to keep his muscles in good condition than a couple of hours good, strenuous football? Help. For Runcorn.

Evidently some a our local club's players do not mean to lie dormant for a whole season, and showed their "love' of the game" by going to the assistance of Runcorn, who on Saturday were opposed to St.. Helens Recs. They were Ben Jolley (back), J. Tranter and J. Baker (three-quarters), and each proved a tower of strength to the Cestrians. Indeed, it was at the hands—or, rather, feet—of Tranter, who dropped a lovely goal—the only points scored in the game—that Runcorn had the novel ex- perience of tasting the sweets of victory, and this was quite a Change to their supporters, who witnessed them go through the whole of last season without registering a victory to their credit.

All the other Northern Union games were interesting encounters, and the value of local talent was demonstrated in nearly all the matches. That is a feature which this unique football season is bringing to light, and junior players—at least those who are having the experience of seasoned players to back them up —are proving that officials of clubs need no loner search 'sn pastures new" for "star artistes "—they are evidently at hand.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

LAST SATURDAY'S RESULTS. Stockton Heath, St points; Bolton, 2, St. James' 12; Whitecross, 0. Wycliffe, 3; Sacred Heart, 0. Parochial, 18; Beamont, 0. Heathside, 7;' St. Mary's, 0.

It will be seen that in some of the matches in last Saturday's Intermediate League heavy scoring took place by the winning team, Ivhiie the five losing teams bagged only two points among then; and these- were taken by Bolton at the expense of Stockton Heath. The Paro- chial team beat Bezunont to the tune of 18 points to none, and St. James' got the better of Whitecross by 12 points to none; but this one-sidedness will doubtless disappear when the men have become more accustomed to playing with each other.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
HEATHSIDE v. ST. MARY'S.

This match, played on the Warrington ground, was an extremely tough struggle be- ' tween two very evenly matched teams. In the first half Heathside showed a slight superior- ity and attacked most of the time, but want of finish spoiled most of their efforts. Harter scored a goal for an infringement and struck the post from another attempt. During the second portion St, liary's attacked strenuously, .at one time penning Heathside on their own goal line for quite ten minutes. On two occasions they should have 'scored easily. The defence of Ileatliside was very good, Griffiths (full-back) playing splen- didly. Near the close Harter, dashing away, scored a try and kicked a goal, thus scoring all the points for his side. St. Mary's were also served well by their back, and their centres were good. They hardly deserved to lose.

Result: Ileathside, 7 points; St. Mary's, nil.

SACRED HEART V. WYCLIFFE.

This match was the opening of the season for both teams and was played on No. 3 .Field in Victoria Park. Wycliffe won the toss and play was carried down to the "Hearts" line, and in the first few minutes Wycliffe gained a try from a scramble on the line, no goal re- sulting. Half-time: Wycliffe, 3 points; Sacred Heart, nil. On resumption play was again forced to near the "Hearts" line, but Leah, intercepting finely, ran more than half the length of the field, and the Wycliffe line was hard pressed. From a scrummage W. Titchard was almost over, and Appleton also had hard lines at the corner. Clarke and Barnett, the respective full-backs,, were always safe. The whistle blew with a victory for Wycliffe by one try to nil.

HARD TIMES.

No Prizes At Secondary School Swimming Gaza.

The Wal,dagton Secondary School held their fourth annual swimming gala at the Public Baths on Wednesday afternoon. There was -a record numliier of entries, the style of the swimming sfingVing no decline felsto previous years. The large attendance of scholars and friends muss have been very gratifying to the members of the staff. Owing to the present demand for egonomy no prizes were awarded, but certificates were presented to the winners by Miss Clariie, the senior mistress. The respective champions this year were found in Elsie Hatch and John Farrington. Results:— .

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON 'WORKING MEN'S

ASSOCIAIION.

N.A. Centre.

The best sport last week-end was the Gow3r, in the upper reaches, where dace were taken up to 9-1 oz. About 50 roach and dace, ranging from 4oz. to 10oz. were taken by five anglers. Chester Centre has already recorded trout 11b. 150 z., dace 12oz. 15dr., gudgeon 2 oz., from these waters, and the Warrington members who are visiting this spot are having fair sport week after week..

THE ANNUAL MATCH.

T4e animal match that was fished at Moulton on September 12th was a great success for the society, as more novices were amongst the prize- winners than there have been for any previous annual. The match was fished in fine weather, and competitors had fair sport. There was a strong current, that was helped by the wind blowing down stream. Time, two hours' fishing. The following is the result:- 1, W. Hewitt, 11b. 7oz. 15dr.; 2, J. Higham, lib. 6oz. Bdr.; 3, B. Bradbury, 11h. soz. 2dr.; 4, T. Chadwick, lib. 4oz. 12dr.; 5 and 6, T. Dixon and W. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 25, 1915.

STORIES FROM THE RA KS.,

A "HO i Gil TIME"

For Warrington Territorial Machine Gun Section.

THE SLACKER'S FOLLY.

Sergeant E. J. Widocks, machine gun section, Warrington Territorials, writing last Thursday to councillor Henshall, Sunnyside, Latchford, says:— _ _ _ . _

• "Now I have goeback 'home' again I grasp this opportunity of writing you a few lines, in the hope of the same finding you quite well. Am glad to be able to tell you that I am quite 0.K., which; being interpreted, Means—l am ,well. Myself and the gunners have been away in the trenches for 19 days, and we have had a very rough time—in fact, the worst time we have, so far, had. The elements, as well as the Allemands, must have conspired to lead us a dance, and right well they carried out their programme. If you can picture Lousher's-lane at its very worse—well, it was a cricket pitch at Lords compared with the trenches. I and several others found enough clay on our cloth- ing to mould a life-size statue of friend Bill! Did we grouse? Not at all., The usual 4th smile was there just. the same, and we just lit our 'Tommy's cooker and made some tea and drank to the speedy downfall of the 3ltad Mullah—beg pardon I I mean the Kaiser. Well, sir, everything comes to an end, and here. we are again.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
A Spot Slackers Should Visit.

"I am sorry to say I lost one of my cor- porals the very last night. He got' a bullet wound in the head and never regained , con- sciousness. I can assure you that we are all sorry, and feel his death very keenly, as he was such a good lad. He lies at rest among some more dead heroes of the old battalion—a spot that I would like the slackers to visit, just to see the resting-place of the lads who were tried and not found wanting. Talking of slackers, I see by the Warrington papers (which we so much look for on Tuesdays) that you have had a recruiting campaign—illuminated cars, bands, wounded soldiers, &c., being the chief attrae- tion—after the speakers, of course. Well, sir, I don't like that way Of getting soldiers. You get their bodies, but not their hearts. I would adopt sterner measures myself. If they gave me a dozen men and a free hand what a haul I could make in Warrington! What a stock of low shoes and heliotrope hose I could gather! Never mind, we are still in good spirits and perfectly willing to do what they are shirking. What would they do if the Huns landed in England and seized their homes, wives, mothers and sisters? Would that awaken them from their Rip Van Winkle-like sleep? Surely it would. I will not say any more about them, but shall continue to pray that they may be led to see their folly. Pined Over Pineapple For Tea.

"I received your box of fruity &c. quite safely, and I and my team of five sat down to a very enjoyable tea in the trench. I think we all pined for home at the time, and, now I think of it, I do believe we were eating the tin of pineapple ! ! It caused us to look on the " Sunny-side " of life as well. "The Battalion are still engaged on very im- portant work, and are doing it in the style that they are noted for-,-first class. Of course, we are looked on as 'old sweats out here, and everyone has a good word for us. When- ever we meet another regiment it is always 'Cheer oh! the 4th.' You see, sir, talent will out.

"I see that you have adopted post women in the old town. I wonder if that is the thin end of the wedge. Never mind, as long as it means more men out here. Of course, if they should happen to 'find seats on the Council—well, 'nough said. Of course, they may have already found seats on some Councils (not Warrington). This is not sarcasm.

"I sincerely hope that you, Mrs. Henshall and son are, Well. Please give them my kind regards—als6 to all friends. With all best wishes for the future. I will close and remain

"Yours sincerely,

"E. J. WIDOCKS."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
"A HOT SHOP."

Warringtonian's Strenuous Time In

Flanders.

Drummer J. J. Brookes, Lancashire Fusiliers, writing to us regarding "Warrington Soldiers in Flanders,':, says :-- " We are aepresent in a hot shop in Flanders. It is to our credit after ten months' severe training in England to be rushed in the firing line for 24 hours' experience to see what trench fighting was really like. We had a pretty rough time of it too, losing a few men. Our next turn in the trenches was for 21 days in the same place, which was a record for holding our line without a relief. To our sorrow we suffered pretty heavily as regards casualties. We had to bring in our own rations at night-time, being constantly shelled by the Germans, whilst working knee deep in mild and water. My worst experience was on the sth September, when we• were again warned for the firing line. We had to take cover in a barn, which was 'near to us, as a heavy bombardment was taking place. One of the German shells (` coal-box ') dropped through the roof into the midst of us. killing 'l4 and wounding 33 of our comrades. I myself, thank God, got out of the barn without a scratch."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD NEWS SOON!

Sergeant Oxley's Cheering Message

From The Front.

Sergeant J. Oxley, of 152, Longford-street, is serving " somewhere " with the 119th Heavy Battery R.G.A., having foregone his work at Messrs. Rylands, Dalton Bank, in favour of military service. In a letter to Mr. B. Farrar (secretary of the Wycliffe P.S.A.), he says:— "The day I received your letter our battery was just starting away to new quarters, about fifty miles further up the line. • Of course, I read it, and being so busy, I put it aside and completely forgot all about it. I watt turning some of my things up yesterday when I sud- denly dropped across your letter, so now I can answer it. Oh, yes, I was home on leave in August, and was married. I heartily thank you for your kind wishes for my wife and myself.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
"Of Course We Grumble."

"I was a member of the P.S.A. in days gone by, and I would certainly have given you all a call when I waS in Warrington, but I am sorry to say that I did not have a Sunday at home. Perhaps the time is not far away though, when I will be able to have a Sunday afternoon amongst you—at least I hope so. I would not mind if it was next Sunday myself. Still, we must be patient; that day will come along right enough. Things are getting pretty lively here at present, and we all think it is quite time to.

Of course we grumble here because we think it is a bit too slow, but we know that the people who are at the head of affairs know more about it than we do. Still, so long as we have our grumble we are perfectly satisfied.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
Finished Before Winter?

"Well, Mr. Farrar, I expect you will hear some startling news from the Front before many days are past; and what is more, it will be good news. Many people here have an idea that it will all be finished before the winter. I hope they are right—especially if we have another winter like the last one. Finish or not finish, we are well preparing for another winter, and no stone is going to be left un- turned.

"I have only met two Warrington fellow,' since I have been here, but I am very plead to say that one of them was my brother. I should dearly like to drop across our Terri- torials from Warrington, as a lot of uly cld chums are out here with them. The divisign we have been transferred to is compwd entirely of Kitchener's Army, and a fine, smart lot of fellows they are, too! We never dreamed we were going to see such a fine lot fellows as these, and I can assure you they opened our eyes a bit. We are proud to be with them, let alone fight with them. " Everrthing is going on just as it ought to do, and the handling and management of such a big Army is really marvellous.. Everyone seems to be in the pink of condition, and we ate all well looked after.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFE IN EGYPT.

Gunner Slater Says It Is One Lon!) Holiday.

NOW 'A SIGNALLER.

Gunner Slater (son of Mr. Edward Slater, Padgate Nurseries); who, along with, a number of .padgate .youths joined. the hoyal Field Artil- lery, soon after the war broke out, is now a signaller • attached to the headquarter staff. Writing to MS father and mother from Egypt, where..ne is stationed, Gunner Slater says I feel twice as well as ever I did in England. I have got used to the heat. The horses are also becoming acclimatised, and a peculiar thing about them is that all the black horses have turned or are turning nearly the same colour as the sand, especially on the parts most exposed to the sun. I have been inoculated twice against cholera. Of course, some of the men objected, as usual, as it is not compulsory, but I was done.

"You talk about the hardships out here. Why! it is one long holiday. We feel that we would like to be doing more for good old Eng- land. I don't think that the people in the old country realise what a big job we have got in the Chiming Bells,' as we call it, or even what good work our Army and Navy are doing there, but You will hear some good news from this end before long. _ _ _

===== 0002601_19150925_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
A Sand Storm.

." I enclose a novel photograph of myself, taken on a camel's back. It is a funny experience to be sitting on a hump, especially when the creature is rising up off the ground. You have to get a good grip fore and aft,' or you are tossed over its head! We are having a sand- storm to-day, and it is a good job we are only about 20 yards from the sea; and, as the wind is blowing from this quarter, we don't get much sand. You can hardly imagine what it is like a mile or so inland. It looks just like•clouds of smoke from here. It is most unpleasant to be in, especially if you have to face it, and both men and horses suffer from sand colic. The horses wear leather muzzles, when they are not feeding, to stop them getting the sand into their mouths. They root into the sand just as pigs do, if they are not muzzled, and seem to like eating it, probably for the salt it contains. "We had a nice surprise the other day, for our Captain's wife sent us each a nice khaki coyer for the back of our helmets to protect the neck and spine; also two packets of 'woodbines' each and a piece of cake. Was it not good of her? The captain is also very good to us. He will sometimes put down a hundred piastre note (just over a sovereign), and tell the cook to get, some little luxufies for us. Sharks and Bathing.

"'We are not allowed to bathe from noon to ?p.m., but as soon as the latter hour is passed. there is a rush for the water. We see a few sharks, but they have never come after any of our men. We are not fat enough yet! We bathe just behind a shallow reef, and they .only come inside the reef in stormy weather, and then, of course, we don't go in. The native fishermen shoot them on sight, because they spoil their fishing, and our officers have had a pot at them once or twice, but they have not managed to land one yet. The native villages here are very quaint. The houses look quite picturesque with their flat roofs, but the ((imitation in most parts is very bad. You can smell the towns and vil- lages almost as soon as you can see them. You see all kinds of animals- in and around the houses. Goats, dogs, and sheep are very com- mon, and often an Arab horse, a donkey, or a camel has the honour of being tied up at the front door. Besides this lot they have turkeys and chickens running all over the place. You will see, therefore, that to keep their places clean they would have to work, but they seem to prefer the smell to the work, for precious little of the refuse is ever cleared .away. Praying Places. .*

"We used to wonder what the little straw huts were built along the roads for, and also on the desert tracks, but we have found • out now. They are praying places. Most of the people are Mohammedans, and they are sup- posed to pray five times a day, wherever they may be, viz., at daybreak, midday, about three in the afternoon, at sunset, and lastly at night. They sit down on their praying 'mats outside the little huts, wash their feet and then begin to pray. This consists of a motion very much like our physical exercises. They first stretch their arms up to the sky, then drop down on their knees and bend over time after time to kiss ,the ground. Most of the Mohammedan women wear the veil, although a few .have adopted Western customs. You can only see the eyes of the former, and the latter have tried to add to their beauty by all sorts of.marks put on their faces—including tattoo marks and scars. You may guess what they are like. Most of them also wear anklets—mostly of silver. Well, I think I would rather have England and its customs than Egypt at any rate. We are all looking forward to the time when we shall be on our way home again. English, French and Arabic.

"The lads have just been having a good laugh at me. You know we only get paid once a fort- night, and even then we rarely draw our full pay, so are often bard up. Well, to-day (Tues- day) we were all stony-broke, and had no cigarettes. A native came into our tent, how- ever, and spotting a belt which I had made out of a piece of canvas, some brass rivets, and two buckles, offered to purchase it. We had a great palaver, and I eventually got two boxes of cigarettes for it and a great deal of fun. His bit of English and French and a lot of Arabic were matched against •my bit of French and Arabic and a lot of English. If any of the boys want to do a deal now with the natives they put me up to act as interpreter !"

===== 0002601_19150925_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
TO GLORIOUS END.

"War Had To Come," Says Sapper Rye.

Sapper G. C. Rye, in a letter to his .friend, Mr. Ben Farrar, of Willis-street, says:— " I was both pleased and surprised to receive a letter from you a few days ago, and I can honestly tell you that my memory is still fresh to the grand meetings of your Brotherhood which I used to attend. If God spares me to return it will be my first thought to give you a, call. . . . I was sorry to read in a paper I happened to have sent out that you had lost a good brother in Joseph Hackett. I knew him quite well. I went to school with him some years _ago, and my deepest sympathy goes to his relatives; also to your Brotherhood in losing such a good worker. "Well, I must say t4iat whatever mines or goes we must see this war to a glorious end; because I think it had to come sooner or later, and it can only finish one way: That is to beat German tyranny and brutality for ever. We are fighting for a just cause, and by God's help we will conquer."

===== 0002601_19150925_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
"PRISONER OF ZENDA " AT STAR

KINENIA HOUSE.

Be it never so great a labour of love, the film- ing of a book which in this exacting age pre- dominates a great section of our country's literature is—to say the least an exacting task. At the popular Star Kinema during this first week ,half we have, seen how it can. be done. "The, Prisoner of Zenda " belongs to that com- paratively small number of books which might specially have been mapped out for the film, and at all houses large crowds assembled to enjoy and enthuse over this dazzlingly spec- tacular theme of noble love and the thwarting of daring plots to oust a misled king from his throne of Ruritania. It should be noted that during the first half of next week the sequel— " Rupert Of Hentzau "—will be shown- 'ln the written story the sequel is perhaps the more entrancing of the two books, so a promising picture is eagerly anticipated. In the programmes being shown to-day the principal film is one of outstanding merit— "The Goose Girl," full of touching scenes, pretty scenery, and exciting passages.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
" Rupert Of Hentzau " Next Week.

The sequel next week! as already mentioned. In book form "Rupert Of Hentzau" is perhaps even superior to its companion volume—" The Prisoner of Zenda"; so the coming film should not be missed. On Thursday, Friday and Saturday the principal film will be "Whoso Diggeth a Pit"—as the title suggests, a telling drama-. •

LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

LANCASHIRE PLAY AT IHE ROYAL COURT.

"Jenny Omroyd, of Oldham," is this week proving just the right kind of fare for large audiences at the Royal Court Theatre. it is a well-constructed and, at times, quite cleverly written domestic drama, whilst all the while it is full of grip, and the author, who is not named on the programme, is to be congratulated upon as workmanlike a piece of drama as vie have had the pleasure of glimpsing for some time. The piece is played by a company who extract every ounce of effect from the concomi- tants supplied them by their author. Miss Ella Thornton is specially effective in the title role—the woman who, deserted by her husband in favour of another woman, herself finds con- solation in the love of a good man. Mr. Douglas B. Watson, as the 'errant husband, acts with quiet forcefulness, and Mr. J. P. Mcßride, as the other man in the tangle, is all that could be desired. Mr. T. G. Bailey scores a big hit as the Rev. Mr. Dogmat, his ludicrous manner- isms keeping the ouse in roars of laughter all the time he is "on." There is not a weak spot in the cast, and the play is as excellently mounted as it is played. We can promise drama lovers a real treat in a visit to the Royal Court Theatre this week. "The Belle Of New York" Next Week.

That ever-popular musical comedy, "The Belle of New York," now in its seventeenth year of tour—and which was recently revived at the Lyceum and Aldwych Theatres, London, where it played to holding capaeity—will be the attraction at the Royal Court Theatre next week.

The play will be presented by Mr. J. Bannister Howard's principal touring company, which in- cludes max of the artistes who were in the grand revival in London. The scenic properties and exquisite costumes are perfectly new, and

are on a most magnificent scale. The piece cannot fail to be a success by reason of its coffstant succession of lively numbers,* its graceful dances, quaint comedy and capital choruses.

The company, which is at present the old favourite, has been carefully chosen, and in- cludes the well-known comedian Johnnie Scho- field as Blinkie Bill, Mr. Gibb McLaughlin as Uhabod Bronson, and Mr. Philip Leslie as Harry Bronson. Mr. Leslie was the original English exponent of this role. On this ,occasion the part of Violet Grey, the Salvation Army lass, will be taken by Miss Edith Dray- son, who by her charm wins the hearts of all. These talented artistes will be supported by a full and powerful company and augmented orchestra, under the direction of Mr. B. J. Paterson. The play will be presented twice nightly at 6.50 and '9, and there will be a matinee on Monday at 2.30 p.m.
