ROLLICKING REVUE AT THE HIPPODROME.

Manager Jackson this week offers specially tip-top fare at the popular house which he con- trols. The bulk of the programme is taken up by a jolly revue entitled "Odds On "—quite one of the prettiest and funniest of the many revues to which the local entreprenners have treated us during the past year or so. We had begun to think that revues were getting a:trifie hackneyed and wearisome, but we had not seen "Odds On" then! As a genuine cure for that unpleasant ailment generically known as "Down in the mouth," we can safely recommend this "merry and bright" trifle, with its pretty girls, its rollicking , coniedians, its tuneful ditties,. its dainty costumes and its pretty scenery. The cast is a specially strong one, and includes such names to conjure with in the revue-world as Harry Dent—a pantomimist of the first water—Bay Russell—a first-rate "juvenile lead "—Dolly Elsworthy—a capti- vating singer and a winsome actress—and I Fanny Harris—who contributes lavishly to the success ,of .the piece. The principals are ably assisted by a large and particularly well-trained chords', who respond readily to the baton wielded by 10.: Denman Harrison, the, Able musical, direetor.for the revue. 'There.are three variety tuns in addition to the merry. musical melange, and. far and away the cleverest-of the trio is that provided by Little Zaza, whose demonstra- tion of thought-transference and mind-reading fully merits .the description of "wonderful," applied to it on the bills. Zaza's turn alone in our opinion is worth the price charged, &c., &e. The other turns are provided by Lily Denville, a dainty comedienne and a clever sabot dancer, and the Mozelles (Ada and Milicent), who sing, and dance with great success. Next Week Chaplin Competition.

Next week's bill promises to be a very strong one. It will be topped by "The• White Mother," a gripping Western drama, of which the scene is laid in the home of a half-breed. A great Charlie Chaplin Competition will aLso be held. Gipsy Woolf comes with her futurist dancing girls, and there will also appear Harry Taft. comedian; Little Lucie Linda, speciality London dancer; Billy Rogers. "humorous Labour Leader "; Hector and Lolletta, Italian equilibrists, with dog; and Shirley and Ransome, in "One 0' Kitchener's ' —a topical Cockney episode.

"SAFELY GATHERED IN."

Harvest Thanksgivings Warrington And District.

Sunday saw the first of the local services of thanksgiving for the safe ingathering of the harvest—a consummation of supreme import-

ance to the nation in this year of war. There were large and devout congregations at all the churches where the festivals were held, and most of the sacred edifices were adorned with flowers, &c., care being i taken as a general rule that nothing should be utilised font decorative purposes if a better use could be found for it.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
CAIRO-STREET UNITARIAN CHAPEL.

Beneficent Influences Of The Country. Harvest thanksgiving services,o held at the Unitarian Chapel, Cairo-street, on Sunday last, were Targely attended; in fact they were an all- round success: The pulpit, the reading desk, &c., were decorated with flOwers and ever- greens, which, at the close of the evening ser- vice, were distributed among members of the congregation, who undertook to see that they fOund their way into the homes of sick members of the chapel. The sermons, both morning and evening, were preached by the Pastor (the Rev. Irvine Lister). At the evening service he discoursed on "The Eternal Lesson of Nature,". and said that amidst all the glorious developments and achievements of modern times, amidst all the discoveries and inventions of the age, which had added so much to the fulness of life, there was one thing which should cause alarm and dis- appointment, and that was the neglect of the country and its abounding beneficent influences. Those Who Only " Hear" Of Nature.

•So greatly had the mode of national life changed that to-day there was growing up a generation which only knew of the real beauties of Nature as a matter of hearsay. Man's natural home was not the bustling, smoky town, but the country, with its quiet, peaceful meadows, with its hills and valleys—places where there was an inspiration to commune with the Creator of all things. All Nature spoke of God, and though it could not provide a full and complete religion of itself, it was. however, sufficient to bring a man to his knees and to fill him with holy and righteous desires and resolves.

• Special 'anthems were rendered at the services —in the morning "What Though j Trace" (Handel), with Miss Mellor as soloist; and in the evening "Sing a Song of Praise" (Stainer). Mr. Jas.. Muir presided at the organ. •

===== 0002601_19150925_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
BrOLb-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

There were large congregations at the harvest thanksgiving services held at the fold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday, the sacred build- ing being prettily decorated for the occasion with the produce of the field and garden. The preacher in the morning was the Rev. H. T. Dixon, who spoke on the parable of the mustard seed, pointing out that just as that tiny seed grew into a big plant so did the three years' public ministry of Christ grow into a movement which had changed the lives of millions of mankind.

The. Rev. Percy meson preached in the even- ing.

In the morning the anthem was Stainer's "Ye Shall Dwell in the Land," and in the evening "Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem," Miss Brough and Mr. Warburton being the morning soloists, and Mrs. A. Booth the evening soloist. Mr. F. Hickman was at the organ, and the cake- lions wert on behalf of the circuit and church funds.

===== 0002601_19150925_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH.

The Church's Outiol)k On The Nation's

Blessings.

Harvest. thanksgiving services were held at St. John's Presbyterian Church on Sunday, when the preacher was the Rev. Alexander Farquhar, of Manchester.

In the evening the minister chose for his subject "A Nation's Blessings," and spoke from the words in the parable of the unjust steward, "How much owest thou unto my lord?" The preacher said he was not going to discuss the parable, but would call attention to the Church's outlook upon their nation's blessings. In the first place thers were their privileges as a nation. How much did they of that land owe to the God of all mercies as inheritors of constitutional government? They had liberty of conscience and were rich in all agencies for promoting the happiness of all classes, for all of which they were indebted to Christianity, as only a Christian nation would have afforded scope for bringing those concep- tions forth. It was the power of Christianity that broke the chain of the oppressor. How much did they owe to the Lord for the blessing of a pure and reformed faith; for the clearer views brought out anew from the dust of the ages? They also had a free and open Bible— free and open to every man! The world moved on under the illuminating power of the Scrip- turSs. They were apt to forget how absolutely dependent they were upon God. They were dependent upon Him for the harvest which gave them -bread—the staff of life! What they also needed and wished to see was a harvest in the spiritual 'world—a harvest of souls! Let them pray for faithful reapers and for a spiritual quickening. The musical i part of the services was under the charge of Mr. A. G. Lewsey (organist) and an augmented choir, a special feature at the evening service being the rendering of the anthem "0 taste and see!" (by Sir John Goss).

===== 0002601_19150925_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. CLEMENT'S MISSION.

On Thursday evening were commenced the St. Clement's Mission harvest celebrations. Inten- tionally (on account of the war) the decorations were carried out on a restricted scale. A good congregation attended to hear the Rev. A. S. Cameron (vicar of Atherton). and there were also present the Rector ,(Rev. Canon Willis) and the Revs. T. Vickers (curate-in-charge) and J. S. Buckmaster.

Mr. Dell presiding at the organ, the choir rendered well the anthem "All Thy works praise Thee, 0 Lord," the solo parts being taken Mr. A. Beswick and Master H. Beswick. A collection was taken on behalf of the Assistant Clergy Fund. On Sunday the preacher will be the Rev. G. C. Briggs (Latchford).

===== 0002601_19150925_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. PETER'S CHURCII.

Not Easy To Re A Christian.

The thanksgiving at St. Peter's Church com- menced on Thursday evening and will be continued to-morrow (Sunday). Thursday's preacher was the Rev. H. C. Moor, vicar of Earlestown, who drew a comparison between the .preparations for harrestide and the real Christian life. It was not easy for anyone to he a real Christian. The weeds of sin had to he destroyed. What great joy there was when thp fruit of the true spiritual .life was gath- ered in. Even in this time of warfare God was working out His purpose--working that better fruit might eventually be gathered in. It was because of God's lore for them that the people of this nation were able to live their lives in safety; it was by His Grace that the Fleet was protected and was able to keep the enemy from these shores,

The choir, which had been ably trained by Mr. F. Webster, acting-organist and choir- master, rendered the anthem ".0 clap your hands together," Miss Mabel Percival singing the solo part, whilst the quartet passages were sung by Miss Percival, Messrs. H. Lawton and J. Le Roy, and Master Albert Powell.

ST. BARNABAS' CHURCH. What War Has Brought Forth A Hundredfold?

Harvest festival celebrations were commenced at St. Barnabas' Church on Thursday evening and will be continued to-morrow. The sacred edifice has been tastefully decorated for the occasion, special attention having been given

<

RAILWAY FARE ALLOWED.

by the willing band of decorators to the altar, pulpit and font.

The preacher was the Rev. J. 31. Hannah, vicar of Ditton. He took as his text the two words " A Hundredfold," and in the course of an eloquent sermon he made reference to the war and spoke of the manner in which during the last troublons year the strife had brought forth a hundredfold the attributes of patriot- ism and courage.

During the service the choir gave an excel- lent rendering of Caleb Simper's anthem "Bless the Lord!" Special psalms were also sung and appropriate lessons read. The festival will be continued to-morrovf (Sunday), when the Vicar (the Rev. J. H. 0. Hollingworth) will preach in the morning, the Rev. T. P. Owen in the afternoon, and the Rev. E. Downham in the evening.

SALVATION ARMY.

Good .Congregalions In Produce- Bedecked Hall.

Harvest festival services were held at OA Salvation Army Hall, Academy-street, last week-end. On Saturday night the band pro- vided a good programme of music and song, which was followed by a pie supper. Sunday's meetings were conducted by Adjutant and Mrs. Beaumont (the officers in charge of the local work) and were well at- tended. The band and Octette Party contri- buted special harvest music and song. The afternoon meeting took the form of service of praise. The Women's Bible Class connected with the Working Men's Mission, under the leadership of Mrs. Moore, gave a her of of pleasing items, which were well received by a large congregation, the programme in- eluding united songs by the class and solos and recitations by incjividual members. ' The address was given by Mrs. Moore, andl Mrs. Owen, of Penketh, who presided, contri- buted a most interesting reading. The hall was suitably decorated with corn, greenery and flowers, and there was a fine display of seasonable produce, which was dis- posed of at a sale on Monday.

ANOTHER D.C.M. Londoner In South Lancashires Wins Coveted Medal. PRESENTATION AT THE BARRACKS. The square at Orford Barracks was on Monday morning the scene of a little ceremony which was as interesting as it is rare. It was the presentation, in the presence of the troops 1 at present in training in Warrington, of that coveted distinction? the Distinguished Conduct Medal, to a member of the 2nd South Lanca- shires, who, having covered himself with glory at the Front, is now spending a few days' furlough in the old country prior to again going to do his "bit" at the Front. A MODEST HERO. The recipient of .the medal is Sergeant John William Windell—an alert-looking young soldier of twenty-five summers; rather on the small side for a military man, but erect of carriage and exhibiting every evidence of being " as hard as nails," despite his many arduous months in the trenches. He is modest hero. and when the reporters surrounded him on Monday morning shortly before the pri7pd medal adorned his tunic, he contented himself with saying that it was a nice day and the more weather like it the boys at the Front Of the better they would be pleased. As to him- self and the brave deeds which won him the D.C.M. he would not talk, except modestly to disclaim any desire for a fuss. He had, he

Hit In Four Places On Way To Trenches. One of the five soldier sons of Mrs. Lucy Bluck, of 53, Howley-lane, has been wounded in action whilst, serving with the Warrington Territorials. He is Private William Bluck a married man, of 5, Wright-street. He W 513 cii his way to the trenches when the Germans opened fire. One bullet lodged in the tim f his cap, and as he stepped forward another hit him in the left arm, fracturing it. He also received three flesh wounds.' Joined " Terriers " When Call Came. Private Bluck was educated at the National School; Church-street. and at .the St. James' School, Latchford. He joined the Territorials soon after the outbreak of war, having pre- viously' been employed on Howley Quay. Along with two of his brothers, he took part in the

RIFLEMAN T. BATES. Wounded. hospital at Faversham, Kent. He added that he was going on all right. Rifleman Bates joined the Rifle Brigade on September Ist of last year, and went to' the Front in January of the present year. He is twenty-three years of age, and he waaemployed as a plater at the Dallam Forge, where he also Served his apprenticeship. He received the first part of his education at the National 43 hools. but spent the last two years of his schooldays at the Silver-street School. Private Bates' pet hobby is pigeon flying, and birds from his cotes have frequently won races. SAW THE KING. Had It Pretty. Hot, But Turks Had It Hotter.
